Review
Modeling and simulating human behavior in large-scale
emergency evacuations: A comprehensive review
Shiva Moslemi a,∗

Disaster management

Simulation–optimization
a b s t r a c t
Disasters pose persistent threats to human life and infrastructure, making evacuation planning
vital for disaster risk reduction. The effectiveness of such efforts depends on understanding and
accurately modeling human behavior. This study reviews 479 peer-reviewed articles on largescale evacuation planning and management, classifying them along two dimensions: behavioral
factors influencing key authority-issued decisions and modeling approaches incorporating these
dynamics within optimization, simulation, and simulation–optimization frameworks. While demographic and route-choice behaviors are extensively studied, mode selection and road-segment
designation remain largely unexplored. Optimization models commonly assume full- or noncompliance, with limited research that considers partial-compliance, leading to more realistic
solutions. On the other hand, simulation-based studies capture dynamic and uncertain conditions
more effectively but are dominated by rule-based behavioral proxies, particularly for departure
timing and route choice, and typically limit dynamic feedback to route choice alone. Simulation–
optimization approaches, while increasingly adopted, remain largely reliant on parameter-based
behavioral representations rather than empirically calibrated, behavior-oriented coupling. Emphasizing the importance of embedding behavioral heterogeneity in model structures, this review
develops a taxonomy, highlights research gaps, and outlines future directions for behaviorally realistic evacuation models that enhance the effectiveness and practicality of evacuation strategies
in real-world crises.
1. Introduction
Disasters, both natural and human-made, cause widespread loss of life, economic damage, and societal disruption. The International Federation of Red Cross and Red Crescent Societies (IFRC) defines a disaster as an event that overwhelms a community’s
capacity to respond, resulting in significant human, material, and environmental losses (IFRC, 2011). In 2024, the Emergency Events
Database (EM-DAT) (EM-DAT, 2024) recorded 393 disasters from natural hazards, causing 16,753 fatalities and affecting 167.2 million people globally. The increasing frequency and severity of such events highlight the critical need for effective disaster management
strategies to mitigate their impact.

S. Moslemi et al.
Among these strategies, evacuation planning and management is essential for protecting populations during emergencies (Bayram,
2016). A well-coordinated evacuation ensures timely relocation from high-risk areas, minimizing fatalities and congestion (Lindell
et al., 2018). However, real-world evacuations often face challenges such as traffic bottlenecks, overcrowded shelters, and inefficient
coordination, exacerbating disaster impacts. Many of these challenges stem from human behavior, such as panic, hesitation, and
non-compliance with evacuation orders (Perry, 1979b; Drabek, 2018). Understanding and predicting evacuee behavior is essential
for designing robust evacuation strategies.
Evacuation management is a complex socio-technical problem due to its large-scale nature and the severe consequences of decisionmaking errors. The sheer size of the problem, combined with the urgency of decision-making, makes manual planning impractical,
requiring advanced models for decision support. These models must account for various logistical, infrastructural, and operational
challenges. However, the inclusion of human behavior, which is difficult to predict and model accurately, adds another layer of
complexity. Addressing this challenge demands both technical expertise and insights into social dynamics, as crisis-driven human
behavior often deviates from rational models, necessitating a socio-technical approach to evacuation planning and management.
Mathematical models and simulations are widely used to support evacuation planning and management, enabling researchers to
assess different scenarios, allocate resources efficiently, and identify potential bottlenecks. However, these models often oversimplify
human behavior, treating evacuees as rational agents who follow predetermined rules. In reality, behavioral factors such as risk
perception, social influence, and decision-making under stress significantly impact evacuation outcomes (Karampotsis et al., 2024).
The US Department of Homeland Security (DHS) and the Federal Highway Administration (FHWA) emphasize the need to incorporate
realistic behavioral dynamics into evacuation planning and management to improve decision-making and adaptability (FEMA, 1996;
FHWA, 2017).
Historical disasters illustrate the consequences of failing to account for human behavior. During Typhoon Haiyan (Yolanda)
in 2013, panic-driven traffic congestion and overcrowded shelters hindered rescue efforts, disproportionately affecting vulnerable
populations (Group, 2017). Similarly, the 2018 wildfires in Greece and the 2019 Sulawesi earthquake and tsunami saw increased
casualties due to confusion, poor coordination, and delays in evacuation (Kitsantonis et al., 2018; Irfan, 2018).
Integrating behavioral insights into evacuation models remains a challenge across all major modeling paradigms. Optimization
models commonly assume that evacuees either fully comply with or entirely ignore official directives, with only limited research
exploring partial compliance. Simulation-based studies capture dynamic and uncertain conditions more effectively, yet the majority
rely on rule-based behavioral proxies rather than empirically grounded models, and dynamic feedback is typically restricted to
route choice alone. Simulation–optimization approaches, while increasingly adopted, remain largely dependent on parameter-based
behavioral representations, with a critical gap in empirical validation. Across all three paradigms, the complexity of human decisionmaking is often overlooked, limiting the ability of current models to capture its direct impact on evacuation effectiveness (Pel et al.,
2010; Wang and Wallace, 2022; Aldahlawi et al., 2024).
This study specifically targets large-scale or mass evacuations from urban areas, which differ from site-specific facility evacuations
in buildings, stadiums, or industrial sites. Large-scale evacuations involve hundreds of thousands of individuals moving across extensive regional road networks over hours or days under hazardous and dynamically evolving conditions. Accordingly, the modeling
approaches suited to this setting differ from those developed for facility-scale evacuations. This study focuses exclusively on the evacuation phase and does not encompass the subsequent return or recovery stages. The research aims to contribute to the United Nations’
“Climate Action” and “Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)” (United Nations Department of Economic and Social Affairs, 2015),
as well as the “Sendai Framework for Disaster Risk Reduction (SFDRR)” (United Nations Office for Disaster Risk Reduction (UNDRR),
2015), by enhancing community resilience and reducing vulnerability to disasters.
Within this defined scope, this survey is motivated by the significant need to incorporate human behavior aspects into large-scale
evacuation planning and management models, as current models often overlook or simplify these critical factors. Despite growing
efforts in this area, numerous reviews have examined various facets of evacuation modeling, including optimization formulations,
traffic simulation, routing algorithms, agent-based social behavior, and emergency logistics. To position this review against prior work,
Table 1 compares representative reviews that address emergency evacuation at large or mixed scale and that contain a substantive
treatment of evacuee behavior. Reviews restricted exclusively to building or confined-space egress are excluded, as they fall outside
the scope defined in Section 2.1.
The comparison is organized around five groups of dimensions. Review scope characterizes each review through its primary subject,
whether evacuee behavior is its main organizing focus or is treated within a broader modeling or planning framework, whether it
synthesizes influencing factors, behavioral modeling, or both, and the scale and hazard context addressed. Behavioral decisions records
coverage of the six authority-issued decisions examined in this study: protective action, departure time, mode choice, destination
choice, route choice, and road-segment designation. Methodological coverage records the extent to which optimization, simulation,
and simulation–optimization studies are systematically reviewed. Behavioral treatment then concerns how behavior is handled within
these methodological streams, covering whether influencing factors are synthesized generally, for specific decisions, or both; whether
the sources of behavioral evidence are identified; whether the review examines how behavior is represented within models; and
whether it addresses compliance with authority-issued instructions. Simulation-specific characterization applies to reviews covering
simulation and records whether the reviewed studies are classified by movement mode, by simulation scale, by simulation method
or tool, and by structural coupling, that is, by how influencing factors, behavioral decisions, and simulated movement are connected
within a model and whether conditions arising during movement inform subsequent behavior.
Read across these dimensions, prior reviews show a consistent pattern of partial coverage. Although most address multiple hazards
rather than a single hazard context, and evacuee behavior is the primary organizing focus in most of them, this breadth does not
extend to the full set of decisions through which evacuation behavior is expressed: decision coverage clusters around protective

action, departure time, destination choice, and route choice, while mode choice appears in fewer than half of the reviews and roadsegment designation in none. Coverage of modeling paradigms is similarly partitioned, with simulation the most widely reviewed
paradigm and optimization the next, yet fewer than half of the reviews cover both and none treats simulation–optimization as a distinct
methodological stream. The depth of behavioral treatment varies in the same way: most reviews examine how behavior is represented
within models and a majority identify the sources from which behavioral evidence is drawn, but none classifies its reviewed studies
according to how compliance is treated or modeled, or by degree of compliance, so compliance remains a recurring observation
rather than an organizing dimension. Among the reviews that address simulation, classification by simulation method or tool and by
simulation scale is common, whereas none characterizes how influencing factors, behavioral decisions, and simulated movement are
structurally linked within a model, including whether these links are unidirectional or involve feedback. No existing survey therefore
combines, within a single framework, a mapping of behavioral factors across all authority-issued evacuation decisions at large scale,
a treatment of compliance and non-compliance as a structuring dimension, a systematic account of how behavioral information is
obtained and represented, and coverage of the three paradigms through which behavior is incorporated into evacuation models.
To address this gap, this review develops a taxonomy linking general behavioral factors and their effects on critical authorityissued evacuation decisions, as well as on compliance with and deviation from them, and provides a comprehensive assessment of how
these behavioral dimensions are obtained and represented across optimization, simulation, and simulation–optimization frameworks.
By reviewing evacuee behavior in large-scale emergencies across these three complementary methodological streams, this study
highlights critical gaps in current evacuation models and proposes key improvements to enhance large-scale evacuation planning and
save lives.
The paper is structured as follows. Section 2 details the methodology, including the selection of studies and analytical framework.
Section 3 explores the behavioral factors influencing evacuation dynamics. Section 4 reviews optimization-based evacuation models
incorporating human behavior. Section 5 reviews simulation-based evacuation models with explicit behavioral representation. Section 6 discusses simulation–optimization approaches designed to enhance evacuation processes. Section 7 examines the empirical
validation of behavioral assumptions. Finally, Section 8 highlights critical research gaps and offers directions for future study.
2. Methodology
We begin with a broad and inclusive search using the general keyword ‘evacuee behavior’ to capture studies across a wide range of
disciplines. Building on this comprehensive foundation, we then refined our focus to identify the specific behavioral factors addressed,
as well as the solution methodologies and their subclasses that incorporate these behavioral effects into qualitative and quantitative
analyzes.
To ensure a transparent evaluation of evacuation strategies, we propose a hierarchical classification framework that categorizes
key evacuation factors. This framework organizes influencing factors into general factors and decision-related factors and classifies
solution methodologies into optimization-based, simulation-based, and simulation–optimization based (S–O) methods. Additionally,
the framework includes important topics such as human behavior, infrastructure constraints, and evacuation rules to show how these
factors are connected and how they help solve key problems in evacuation planning and management.
2.1. Paper selection process
This review followed the PRISMA (Preferred Reporting Items for Systematic Reviews and Meta-Analyzes) framework to ensure
a transparent and reproducible study identification, screening, and selection process. In line with this framework, we searched two
major bibliographic databases, Scopus and Web of Science, to identify studies published in English between 2000 and 2025. The search
was limited to articles and review papers. To reduce the risk of selection bias and capture the breadth of the literature on evacuation
behavior, the search strategy was organized around three concept blocks related to evacuation context, behavioral constructs evacuee
decision-making and response, and modeling approaches. The following Boolean search query was used across all databases:
(Evacuat* OR “shelter-in-place” OR sheltering OR “emergency evacuation” OR “large-scale evacuation” OR “household evacuation” OR “neighborhood evacuation”) AND (“evacuee behavior” OR “evacuation intention” OR compliance
OR “protective action” OR “route choice” OR “destination choice” OR “departure time” OR “evacuation timing” OR
“mode choice” OR “risk perception” OR “warning response” OR “evacuation behavior” OR “evacuation decision” OR
preparedness OR “travel behavior” OR “sheltering behavior” OR “routing” OR “evacuation guidance” OR “evacuation
demand” OR “staging”) AND (“modeling” OR “behavioral modeling” OR “behavioral modeling” OR “optimization” OR
“mathematical programming” OR “mixed integer linear programming” OR MILP OR “linear programming” OR LP OR
bilevel OR “bi-level” OR “stochastic programming” OR “simulation” OR “agent-based” OR “mathematical model” OR
“transportation modeling” OR “choice model” OR “logit model” OR “traffic assignment” OR “shelter location”)
The database search yielded 3041 records in total, including 1572 from Scopus and 1469 from Web of Science. Fig. 1 summarizes
the identification, screening, eligibility assessment, and inclusion stages of the study selection process.
All retrieved records were imported into Zotero, a bibliographic reference management software, to organize the records and
identify duplicate entries. After removing 1043 duplicates, 1998 unique papers remained for screening; this deduplicated set is
available as a public Zotero library at https://www.zotero.org/groups/6651845/large-scale_emergency_evacuation_behavior_review/
library.These studies were eligible for inclusion if they addressed emergency evacuation and incorporated an explicit behavioral
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
4

S. Moslemi et al.
Fig. 1. PRISMA flow diagram of the paper selection process.
component within simulation, optimization, or related analytical modeling frameworks. Studies were excluded if they fell outside
the evacuation domain; did not include a substantive behavioral component; focused exclusively on building-scale or other confinedspace evacuations; examined post-disaster or recovery-phase behavior rather than behavior arising before or during evacuation; or did
not concern emergency evacuation. This scope boundary applies to modeling paradigms as well as to settings. Pedestrian dynamics
models, including social force and cellular automata formulations, are included when applied to large-scale outdoor urban evacuation
and excluded when used to represent building egress or confined-space crowd flow. Title and abstract screening was then conducted
using ASReview LAB, a machine-learning-assisted screening platform. Approximately 500 records were manually labeled on the basis
of the eligibility criteria, after which the trained model was used to prioritize the remaining records according to predicted relevance.
The resulting ranking was used only to support the order of screening and improve efficiency. After title and abstract screening, 386
papers from the database search were retained. Papers retained at this stage were then subjected to full-text review, during which the
eligibility criteria were applied. Four papers whose full texts could not be accessed were excluded at this stage. In addition, a total of
145 papers were excluded for reasons such as confined, local, or small-scale evacuation cases; the absence of an explicit evacuation
movement or behavioral component; a non-emergency context; lack of focus on evacuation; or failure to use the target method. We
then conducted backward snowballing (Hirt et al., 2024), that is, screening the reference lists of retained papers to identify further
eligible studies, identifying 242 additional studies that met the same criteria. As a result, the final sample comprised 479 papers,
including 237 papers identified through database searching and 242 papers added through backward snowballing.
2.2. Paper classification process
After completing the paper selection process, the retrieved studies were categorized into two groups: (1) factors influencing
evacuee behavior and (2) solution methodologies. Although there were 485 entries across the two categories, six papers overlapped,
resulting in 479 distinct papers. Fig. 2 presents the classification framework used to organize and categorize the included studies.
The influencing factors category consists of 295 papers examining the various factors affecting evacuee decision-making during
evacuation. These factors are broadly grouped into two main categories: General and Decision-Related. The General category includes
172 studies and is further classified into two subcategories: environmental conditions and social context, and personal characteristics.
Environmental concerns include the severity of the disaster, disaster occurrence times, weather conditions, infrastructure, and the
physical environment, which are in total examined in 77 papers. Social concerns, such as social networks, cultural beliefs and norms,
and language barriers, were discussed in a total of 64 papers. Additionally, information-related factors, including sources, channels,
and warning messages, were analyzed in 35, 34, and 41 papers, respectively.
Personal characteristics in evacuation behavior encompass a wide range of factors, including physical and psychological traits,
socioeconomic status, family context, past disaster experience, demographics, and risk perception. Physical and psychological traits
were examined in 75 papers, while socioeconomic factors, including income level and access to resources, were examined in 44 papers.
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
5

S. Moslemi et al.
Fig. 2. Paper classification framework.
Family context, particularly the presence of dependents like children and pets, was analyzed in 65 papers, and past experiences with
disasters were considered in 51 papers. Demographic factors, including age, gender, education level, and length of residence, were
discussed in 86 papers, whereas risk perception, which shapes individual responses to evacuation warnings, was studied in 18 papers.
While a total of 172 papers were reviewed for general influencing factors, many studies examined multiple factors, leading to a
cumulative count of 590 mentions. This highlights the complexity of evacuation decision-making, where environmental, social, and
personal factors interact in diverse ways to influence individual responses. Building on these influencing factors, we next examine
decision-related factors, focusing on how evacuees respond to key evacuation decisions.
The decision-related factors are investigated in 213 papers that specifically examine how evacuees respond to key evacuation
decisions made by authorities, considering the influencing factors identified in the first category. Notably, 20 of these papers appear
in both the general and decision-related subcategories, as they address how broader environmental, social, or personal factors affect
evacuees’ responses to specific decisions. These studies are categorized by the specific type of decision addressed and examine individuals’ compliance, their alternative actions in cases of noncompliance, and the reasons behind their behavior. One of the key decisions
examined is which protective action to take, such as whether to evacuate or shelter in place, which is discussed in 71 papers. Another
major focus is departure time, which is a critical factor influencing evacuation efficiency and congestion. Departure time is analyzed
in 38 papers. Transportation mode decisions, including whether evacuees are advised to use personal vehicles, public transit, or walk,
were addressed in 28 studies. Destination selection, which concerns public shelters designated by authorities, was examined in 53
papers. Route selection, covering both pre-trip decisions and dynamic adjustments made during evacuation, was the most frequently
studied topic, appearing in 63 papers. In contrast, the road segment designation decision, which refers to the assignment of road
segments to manage mixed pedestrian–vehicle traffic during evacuation, was the least studied, with only two papers addressing this
issue.
The solution methodologies category includes 190 papers on optimization methods, simulation, and simulation–optimization
approaches, focusing on prescriptive rather than descriptive analyzes. Notably, two papers are common across these three categories.
Unlike standalone simulation studies that offer insights into evacuee behavior, these methods develop actionable strategies to improve
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
6

S. Moslemi et al.
evacuation planning and management. By integrating optimization techniques, they enhance decision-making efficiency, optimizing
resource allocation, route planning, and shelter assignments to improve overall evacuation outcomes.
Our search has identified a total of 61 papers that incorporate human behavior into large-scale evacuation optimization models.
As shown in Fig. 2, these papers can be categorized according to their approach to compliance behavior. Of the 61 unique papers,
50 explicitly incorporated compliance behavior into optimization models, seven considered the determinants (drivers) of compliance
behavior, and four provided general insights without direct model integration. Among the 50 model-based papers, 46 addressed route
assignment, 17 focused on destination selection, 17 examined departure time, and only two analyzed protective actions, specifically
compliance with evacuation orders as reflected in participation rates. Several papers considered multiple decision types; therefore,
the counts across decisions exceed the number of unique papers. The majority of papers directly integrating compliance behavior
into optimization models rely on classical approaches: 25 adopt system-optimal (SO) or constrained system-optimal (CSO) to assume
full compliance, 16 use user-equilibrium (UE) or its extensions to capture selfish decision-making, and only nine explicitly model
partial compliance, where evacuees follow instructions to varying degrees. Among the seven papers examining the determinants of
compliance behavior, one addressed personal ability, another focused on psychological traits, the third considered a combination of
personal and psychological characteristics, and four on structural plan/design features. The details of this classification are discussed
in Section 4 providing insight into compliance-related modeling in evacuation optimization and highlighting key research gaps for
future studies.
We also identified 62 simulation-based evacuation papers that explicitly represent behavioral components in large-scale evacuation studies. These papers were systematically reviewed and classified. Among them, three papers discuss the importance of
integrating human behavior into simulation models. Among the remaining 59 papers, 50 integrated both movement representation and behavioral components, while the remaining nine were human-behavior-centric and focused on behavioral responses
without explicitly simulating movement. In terms of movement representation, microscopic simulation was the most common approach (34 papers), followed by mesoscopic (seven), macroscopic (six), hybrid micro–meso (two), and hybrid meso–macro (one)
approaches. Across behavioral dimensions, these studies represented key evacuation decisions, including route choice (55), departure time (38), destination choice (36), protective action (35), and compliance with evacuation-related decisions (30). The
most common input factors were risk perception and information (39) and social influence (19). Because a single paper may address multiple behavioral dimensions, and many movement-based studies also incorporated behavioral components, these counts
are not mutually exclusive. The details of this classification, together with the key findings and research gaps, are discussed in
Section 5.
There are 69 papers that incorporate simulation–optimization approaches in evacuation studies. Among these, 41 papers utilized the Iterative Simulation–Optimization (ISO) method, which involves continuously refining simulation outputs through iterative
optimization. Another 13 papers applied the Iterative Optimization–Simulation method (IOS), where optimization is conducted iteratively with updated simulation results. Additionally, two papers explored the Sequential Simulation–Optimization (SSO) method,
where simulation–optimization is conducted in a stepwise manner rather than in an iterative loop. Only one paper examined the
Alternating Simulation–Optimization (ASO) method, which suggests a different approach to integrating these two techniques. Furthermore, 12 papers were review studies that provided an overview and comparative analysis of these methodologies. These findings
highlight the diversity of simulation–optimization approaches applied in evacuation research. A more detailed discussion of these
methods, along with their advantages and limitations, is presented in Section 6. Next, we examine how human behaviors influence
evacuation outcomes and affect the overall effectiveness of evacuation planning.
The following subsection details the primary factors influencing evacuee decision-making during emergencies.
3. Evacuee behavior during disasters
Evacuation decisions during natural disasters result from a dynamic interaction between external and internal factors. External
influences include environmental conditions such as disaster severity, official warnings, and social pressures, while internal aspects
encompass personal attributes like risk perception, past experiences, and emotional responses. Understanding these interactions is
essential for accurately modeling evacuee behavior and improving emergency evacuation management strategies (Dash and Gladwin,
2007; Lindell and Perry, 2012).
These factors, by shaping individual behavior, critically impact the effectiveness of evacuation planning and management. Authorities play a central role in evacuation management by issuing orders, setting departure timelines, and designating destinations, routes,
and transportation modes. However, some evacuees deviate from these directives due to factors such as perceived risks, awareness
levels, distrust in official guidance, past experiences, psychological influences, and sociodemographic characteristics. These deviations can lead to unexpected changes in evacuation patterns, increasing congestion, elevating safety risks, and complicating overall
coordination.
These behavioral variations create challenges for evacuation planning and management, necessitating a deeper understanding of
how evacuees respond to official directives. This section examines both the key factors influencing evacuee behavior and their impact
on people’s responses to evacuation-related decisions. It explores how individuals react under these influences and what alternative
actions noncompliant evacuees take. In addition, the underlying reasons for these alternative choices are analyzed. By examining these
behavioral patterns, this section highlights the complexity of human responses and the challenges they pose for effective emergency
evacuation planning and management.
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
7

S. Moslemi et al.
Fig. 3. Overview of human behavior research.
3.1. Factors affecting evacuee behavior
During evacuations, panic and stress often trigger unpredictable behavior, leading individuals to disregard official directives
(Quarantelli, 1980). Recognizing the behavioral drivers behind such actions is crucial for improving evacuation planning and management (Ibrahim et al., 2016). A better understanding of these tendencies enables the design of strategies that facilitate safe and
orderly movement.
According to Lindell et al. (2018), Ibrahim et al. (2016), the factors influencing evacuation decisions are grouped into three
overarching categories: Environmental context, social context, and personal characteristics. Environmental factors include disaster
severity, time-of-occurrence, weather, infrastructure, and physical environment. Social factors include social networks, cultural norms,
language barriers, and information dissemination (e.g., sources, channels, and warnings). Personal characteristics encompass physical
and psychological traits, socioeconomic status, family context, prior-disaster experience, demographic attributes, and risk perception.
A visual summary of this classification is provided in Fig. A.1 in Appendix A. Additionally, Table 2 presents a summary of these factors,
detailing their influence across different phases of the evacuation process. It includes each factor’s impact on behavior, whether it
increases or decreases evacuation readiness, the timing of its influence (e.g., onset or during evacuation), and relevant behavioral
correlations.
To uncover patterns in evacuation behavior and identify which factors are well-studied versus underexplored, Table B.1 in Appendix B provides a synthesis. This overview is based on an analysis of 172 studies examining factors that influence evacuation
decisions and reveals three key trends in current disaster research.
Fig. 3(a)–(c) summarize the geographical distribution of the studies, the disaster types investigated, and the behavioral factors examined. As shown in Fig. 3(a), more than half of the reviewed studies (61%) were conducted in the United States. This concentration
reflects substantial national investment in disaster research, but it may also reflect broader publication and funding asymmetries, and
should not be read as indicating an absence of relevant work in other regions. Fig. 3(b) indicates a strong emphasis on hurricanes
(47%), whereas research on no-notice disasters remains limited, even though they account for 73% of disaster-related fatalities (EMDAT, 2024). Fig. 3(c) highlights that demographic characteristics (15%) and environmental concerns (13%) are the most frequently
studied behavioral factors. While psychological and sociological studies have long explored these dimensions, their integration into
operational evacuation models remains limited. Most existing models prioritize logistical elements such as traffic flow and infrastructure, without adequately addressing the complexity of human behavior. Furthermore, the literature is predominantly centered on
disasters occurring in the Northern Hemisphere, pointing to the need for geographically and contextually diverse research to better
inform global evacuation strategies.
3.1.1. Environmental and social context
To better understand how human behavior influences evacuation outcomes, we examine the key categories that shape individual
decision-making during disasters. Building on the previously outlined classification, this section first focuses on the environmental
and social context, which encompasses external factors that influence individuals’ risk perception and evacuation decisions (Huang
et al., 2016a). These factors are organized into several subcategories, each contributing in distinct ways to evacuee responses under
emergency conditions.
• Environmental concerns: severity of disaster is a critical factor in decision-making processes. Studies show that 40–60% of people evacuate when they perceive a direct threat or receive a mandatory evacuation order, demonstrating that immediate danger
strongly impacts behavior (Bian et al., 2022a; Adjei et al., 2022, Bakhshian and Martinez-Pastor, 2023). Individuals often rely
on visible environmental cues as indicators of danger (Lindell, 2012). In the absence of such cues, confusion can arise, leading
to hesitation or reluctance to evacuate. Physical environment, such as geography, infrastructure, and road networks, also plays a
significant role (Noltenius, 2008, Troncoso Parady and Hato, 2016, Ersing et al., 2020, Iwai, 2026). For instance, New Orleans’

geography complicates evacuation logistics, requiring strategies like contraflow traffic (Wolshon, 2002). Weather and season further impact behavior, influencing preparation and travel times. Extreme conditions, such as winter or severe weather, can slow
evacuations and increase stress (Fu et al., 2007; Pham et al., 2020; Sakamoto et al., 2020; La Greca et al., 2023). Time of day affects
evacuation logistics; daytime evacuations face challenges like traffic congestion, while nighttime evacuations, though quicker due
to less traffic, suffer from reduced visibility (Stern, 1989; Lu et al., 2014). Although both daytime and nighttime have challenges,
studies on Hurricane Ike indicate that more people tend to evacuate during the daytime (Huang et al., 2012). Infrastructure and
resources, including route familiarity and shelter availability, also shape decision-making (Dombroski et al., 2006, Lambert et al.,
2013, Sadri et al., 2014b, Anyidoho et al., 2023; Mapa et al., 2026). Studies have shown a preference for private accommodations
over public shelters during evacuations (Perry, 1979b; Baker, 1995; Whitehead et al., 2000; Cheng et al., 2008a; Yang et al., 2016;
DiGrande et al., 2019, Morooka and Yamada, 2017; De Maio, 2013; D’Amico et al., 2024; Wang et al., 2020; Guan and Chen,
2021).
• Social concerns: Social network, where observing neighbors evacuate can prompt others to follow, driven by herd behavior (Baker,
1995; Riad et al., 1999; Kang et al., 2007; Horney et al., 2010, Jiang et al., 2021; Jin et al., 2025). Herd behavior, the tendency to
imitate nearby actions, affects evacuation decisions and route choices (Mawson, 2005; Song et al., 2006; Adiga et al., 2013, Intini
et al., 2019; Zhang et al., 2024b). The influence of this behavior increases with the number of surrounding individuals (Kinateder
et al., 2018). Understanding this behavior can improve emergency response planning (Demiroluk et al., 2016, Aldahlawi et al.,
2024, Reyes-Norambuena et al., 2025). For example, during Hurricane Irma, 64% of evacuees were influenced by the actions
of family members or neighbors, highlighting the importance of collective action in evacuation rates (Kinateder and Warren,
2016; Sadri et al., 2017a; Collins et al., 2018, Ahmed et al., 2020, Xie et al., 2023). Modern analyzes using Twitter and Facebook
data further confirm that social network density directly correlates with higher compliance rates in high-risk zones (Roy and
Hasan, 2021; Rashid et al., 2025). This behavior aligns with findings that approximately 30% of respondents delay evacuation to
confirm warnings through observation of others’ actions or seeking additional information, emphasizing the role of social cues in
decision-making during emergency evacuation planning and management (Lindell and Perry, 2012, Urata and Hato, 2021; Diaz
et al., 2023). Cultural beliefs and norms impact how different communities perceive risk and trust authorities (Fothergill et al.,
1999, Toledo et al., 2018; Wambura and Wong, 2025). For example, Mexican Americans may perceive flood risks as less severe
than white individuals and may be less likely to evacuate (Perry and Lindell, 1991). Cultural ties can also encourage collective
evacuation behaviors within families, as seen in underserved groups where community reliance often substitutes for official aid
(Lindell et al., 2005; Blendon et al., 2007, Wong et al., 2023; Wambura and Wong, 2025). Trust in authorities is critical, as past
discrimination can diminish evacuation compliance among minorities (Elliott and Pais, 2006, Ricchetti-Masterson and Horney,
2013, Kim and Oh, 2015; Ayeb-Karlsson et al., 2019). Language barriers, such as during the 2007 San Diego wildfires, can hinder
evacuation efforts (Lindell et al., 2018), a problem now partially mitigated by SMS alerts and localized digital notification strategies
(Hou et al., 2020; Elejalde et al., 2025).
• Information sources: Information sources refer to the origin of information that individuals trust and consult when deciding
whether to evacuate. Trustworthy sources facilitate timely responses, as observed during Hurricane Lili, where households relied
on reliable authorities to guide their actions (Lindell et al., 2005, McLennan et al., 2019; Ling et al., 2021; Mahittikul et al., 2024).
For instance, during Hurricane Katrina, 69% of early evacuees reported relying on official sources, such as local authorities and
the media, which helped them make informed decisions (Demiroluk et al., 2016; Huang et al., 2017; Sadri et al., 2017b, RoessingAnderson, 2023). Conversely, when individuals lack trust in these authorities or when information is perceived as inconsistent,
skepticism can delay action, creating a “crying wolf” effect that diminishes future compliance (Baker, 1991, Bian et al., 2022b;
Adjei et al., 2022; Tsekeni et al., 2025). This issue is particularly pronounced in areas where declining trust in government has
led people to seek alternative sources, such as peer groups or real-time social media updates, to reduce perceived uncertainty (An
et al., 2015, Walch, 2018, Ling et al., 2021). Recent studies emphasize that the consistency between official warnings and informal
social cues is the most significant predictor of trust during rapid-onset disasters (Ling et al., 2021; Mahittikul et al., 2024).
• Information channels: Information channels represent the mediums through which information from sources is communicated
to the public. While sources identify who delivers the information, channels focus on how the information is delivered. The effectiveness of evacuation communication depends on how well these channels convey credible and accessible information. During
Hurricane Lili, local news media were found to be the most trusted channel, followed by national news, peers, local authorities,
and the Internet (Lindell et al., 2005, Robinson and Khattak, 2010; Hasan et al., 2011; Sadri et al., 2017b). Studies also highlight
that mass media, as a channel, can shape public perception of crises, while social media and mobile platforms can help reduce
immediate concerns by providing real-time, location-based updates (Cheng et al., 2015; Diwanji et al., 2020, Roy and Hasan, 2021;
Han et al., 2023; Elejalde et al., 2025; Rashid et al., 2025). Recent advancements demonstrate that smartphone applications using
GPS-positioning significantly improve route-finding efficiency and reduce evacuation times, especially for individuals unfamiliar
with the local geography (Muthohar et al., 2020; Han et al., 2023). Furthermore, big data analytics from platforms like Twitter
and Facebook allow for the dynamic monitoring of evacuation demand, providing emergency managers with a direct channel to
observe and influence behavioral responses in real-time (Roy and Hasan, 2021; Rashid et al., 2025). Emergency services must
adapt their strategies to demographic and geographical factors to ensure that the best-suited channels, ranging from traditional
sirens to SMS-based alerts, reach the right audiences effectively (Hou et al., 2020; Nadimi et al., 2025; Elejalde et al., 2025).
• Warning messages: Warnings are critical in the evacuation process and must be clear, specific (Bean et al., 2015), timely, and
delivered through multiple channels for maximum reach (Mileti and Peek, 2000, Koshute, 2013, Lazo et al., 2015, Hou et al.,
2020). Effective warnings depend on both content and the medium used to deliver them, ensuring that evacuees understand the
urgency and act accordingly (Perry and Quarantelli, 2005, Xu et al., 2022b; Makinoshima and Imamura, 2024). For instance, 90%
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
10

S. Moslemi et al.
of residents who received clear, specific warnings about Hurricane Harvey’s impact were more likely to follow evacuation orders
compared to vague or generalized messages (Cheng et al., 2013, Gao et al., 2022, Bian et al., 2023; Chen et al., 2024). Recent
studies on phased evacuation orders indicate that compliance is significantly higher when stakeholders’ perceptions of safety
and government responsibility are addressed directly within the message (Bian et al., 2023). Furthermore, the transition from
receiving a warning to taking action such as often delayed by “milling” behavior can be optimized through messages that reduce
perceived uncertainty and provide concrete evacuation triggers (McLennan et al., 2019; Alawadi et al., 2022; Makinoshima and
Imamura, 2024). This emphasizes the need for precise and dynamic communication strategies that adapt to the evolving nature
of the disaster response curve (Koshute, 2013; Bian et al., 2023).
3.1.2. Personal characteristics
Following the discussion of environmental and social influences, we now turn to the second main category shaping evacuation
behavior: Personal characteristics. This category encompasses internal, individual-level factors that play a critical role in how
people perceive risk, process information, and ultimately make decisions during emergency evacuation planning and management.
• Physical and psychological conditions: Disabilities can delay evacuation due to the need for extra time, assistance, or equipment,
posing risks to the individuals and those providing help (Demiroluk et al., 2016, Ducy and Stough, 2021; Hostetter and Naser,
2022, Greer et al., 2023; Reyes-Norambuena et al., 2025). For example, during Hurricane Katrina, 23% of New Orleans’ population
had a disability, yet many were unable to evacuate due to inadequate planning that overlooked their needs. Challenges included
inaccessible transportation, such as buses without wheelchair ramps, and the failure to deploy evacuation resources to areas
with large numbers of disabled residents, revealing systemic barriers in disaster preparedness (Wolshon et al., 2005, Powell
and Gilbert, 2006, Hasan et al., 2012; Reyes-Norambuena et al., 2025). Recent studies utilizing multi-criteria decision-making
(MCDM) frameworks emphasize that integrating human factors into evacuation models is essential for enhancing the preparedness
of these vulnerable populations (Reyes-Norambuena et al., 2025). In large-scale urban evacuations, delays are often attributed
to insufficient transportation planning and a lack of resource allocation rather than physical exit accessibility within buildings
(Christensen, 2011, Wang et al., 2021; Elhami-Khorasani et al., 2023). Poor infrastructure and uncoordinated resource deployment
can exacerbate delays, especially in no-notice events where time-dependent transition probabilities are high (Guan and Chen,
2021). Stress and anxiety can impair decision-making during disasters, potentially causing immobility or disorganized evacuations
(Vorst, 2010, Bernardini et al., 2016; Alawadi et al., 2020; Adjei et al., 2022). Panic, influenced by demographic factors like age
and gender, can lead to unsafe evacuations (Quarantelli, 1975; Li et al., 2017, Hall et al., 2017; Urata and Pel, 2018; Nadimi et al.,
2025). Anxiety and perceived uncertainty can delay evacuation until the threat is perceived as imminent, a phenomenon closely
linked to the “perceived safety” of the chosen evacuation route (Czajkowski, 2011; Adjei et al., 2022; Grajdura and Rowangould,
2025; Ma and Lee, 2025).
• Socioeconomic conditions: Influence individuals’ evacuation capabilities, particularly through the availability of resources (Xu
et al., 2016; Yabe and Ukkusuri, 2020; Chang et al., 2021; Anyidoho et al., 2022). Higher income and education levels are often
associated with a preference for private accommodations, whereas individuals with lower incomes are more likely to depend on
public shelters (Whitehead et al., 2000; Mozumder et al., 2008; Sadri et al., 2014a; Lim et al., 2019b). Additionally, homeownership
may contribute to evacuation reluctance due to emotional attachment to property (Yang et al., 2016, Sarwar et al., 2018; Golshani
et al., 2020), whereas renters demonstrate a 1.33 times higher likelihood of evacuating compared to homeowners (Kulkarni et al.,
2017, Bian et al., 2019; Rambha et al., 2021).
• Family context: Households with children, elderly, or disabled members often require additional preparation and transportation
time, thereby influencing evacuation response times (Perry, 1979b; Lindell and Prater, 2007, Dixit et al., 2012, Huang et al., 2012;
Lim Jr et al., 2022, Sun et al., 2024; Nadimi et al., 2025). Families typically make decisions collectively, which can further delay
their responses (Sadri et al., 2017a; Wong et al., 2023; Golshani et al., 2020). Pets also play a significant role in evacuation decisionmaking, as many pet owners refuse to evacuate without them (Dow and Cutter, 2000, Alawadi et al., 2020; Greer et al., 2023). This
necessitates finding pet-friendly shelters and transportation options, which can introduce delays and increase stress levels (Heath
et al., 2001). Legislative measures such as the Pet Evacuation and Transportation Standards Act (PETS), enacted after Hurricane
Katrina, aim to address these challenges by promoting inclusive evacuation plans (Hunt et al., 2012; Farmer et al., 2016). The
presence of children further shapes evacuation behavior, as parents prioritize their children’s safety. This often includes retrieving
children from schools, despite established procedures, which can lead to significant delays in evacuation timelines (Stern, 1989;
Perry and Lindell, 1991; Baker, 1991; Lindell et al., 2005, Robinson and Khattak, 2010; Hasan et al., 2011; Diaz et al., 2023).
• Past experiences: Past experiences with disasters significantly influence evacuation behavior, often leading to faster and more
decisive actions due to increased awareness and preparedness (Lazo et al., 2015, Wong et al., 2020a; Grajdura et al., 2021).
These experiences shape individuals’ perceptions of risk, trust in authorities, and preferred response strategies (Kinateder et al.,
2015, Hall et al., 2017; Mahittikul et al., 2024). Empirical studies on events such as Hurricanes Lili, Katrina, and Rita reveal
that individuals with prior evacuation experience tend to rely on familiar routes during subsequent evacuations (Wu et al., 2012,
Syamsidik and Istiyanto, 2013; Chen et al., 2022). Notably, approximately 40% of evacuees prefer familiar routes, even when
alternative paths may offer shorter travel time (An et al., 2015, Li et al., 2022b, Bakhshian and Martinez-Pastor, 2023). While
positive past experiences can foster efficient evacuation behavior, negative experiences may lead to hesitation, increased anxiety,
or misjudgments in response-strategies, highlighting the dual impact of past experiences on evacuation decision-making (Huang
et al., 2016a, McLennan et al., 2019; Chen et al., 2021a).
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
11

S. Moslemi et al.
• Demographic factors: Encompass age, gender, education, marital status, employment, and length of residence (Baker, 1991,
Cheng et al., 2011; Raei et al., 2025). Age influences evacuation behavior, with older individuals often evacuating more slowly
or choosing to stay behind (Lindell and Prater, 2000; Cahalan and Renne, 2007; Lazo et al., 2015, Urata and Pel, 2018; Chen
and Cheng, 2020). Gender shapes risk perception and response, with women being 1.36 times more likely to evacuate than men,
as observed during disasters like Hurricane Sandy. Women often prioritize the safety of dependents, while men may stay to
protect property (Fothergill, 1996; Bateman and Edwards, 2002; Neumayer and Plümper, 2007; Kulkarni et al., 2017, Bian et al.,
2019; Kuligowski et al., 2022). Education level enhances risk perception and compliance with evacuation orders, contributing
positively to overall community response (Muttarak and Lutz, 2014; Kim and Oh, 2015; Lindell et al., 2018, Chen et al., 2024;
Wambura and Wong, 2025). Length of residence affects willingness to evacuate; long-term residents often resist due to familiarity
and attachment, while newer residents are more likely to comply with orders. For instance, during Hurricane Floyd, recent
residents were approximately 10% more likely to evacuate than those with over a decade of residence (Baker, 2000, Intini et al.,
2019; Lim et al., 2019b, Wong, 2020). International tourists tend to evacuate readily, relying on social media and local authorities
and facing fewer property constraints. However, their responses may initially be slower due to unfamiliarity with local evacuation
procedures (Charnkol and Tanaboriboon, 2006; Lindell and Prater, 2007; Pennington-Gray et al., 2014; Cahyanto and PenningtonGray, 2015, Iwai, 2026; Raei et al., 2025).
• Risk perception: The subjective judgment of a risk’s severity and likelihood plays a pivotal role in evacuation behavior (Slovic,
1987, Bian et al., 2022a; Li et al., 2022b). This perception is shaped by sociodemographic factors such as age, household size, and
income (Huang et al., 2017, Rana et al., 2020, Ling et al., 2021), as well as by the credibility and frequency of warnings, which can
enhance awareness or cause warning fatigue (Mileti et al., 1975; Mileti, 1999; Trettin and Musham, 2000; Paul, 2012, Adjei et al.,
2022; Bian et al., 2023). Social and cultural filters further influence how individuals interpret the same risk messages (Turner,
1979, Urata and Hato, 2021; Zhang et al., 2024b). Local knowledge is also critical; for example, only 14% of Florida residents
knew their home’s elevation, causing many to underestimate storm surge risks and delay evacuation (Baker and Zhao, 2012,
Guan and Chen, 2021; Tsekeni et al., 2025). Understanding these dynamics is essential for improving public risk-awareness and
increasing compliance with evacuation strategies (Perry, 1994; Gladwin and Peacock, 1997; Riad et al., 1999; Dash and Morrow,
2000; Dash and Gladwin, 2007, An et al., 2015, Lovreglio et al., 2020, Wang et al., 2021; Elhami-Khorasani et al., 2023).
The next subsection groups these factors into compliant and non-compliant responses to evacuation orders.
3.2. Compliance/noncompliance to evacuation orders and alternative evacuee actions
Evacuation planning and management encompasses strategic and operational decisions such as whether to evacuate or shelter
in place, evacuation timing, designated routes, assigned shelter, and transportation mode selection (Bayram and Yaman, 2024).
These decisions are generally made with the expectation of public compliance; however, non-compliance is frequently observed
in practice, posing significant challenges for planners and authorities (Lindell et al., 2018; Wong, 2020). In large-scale, city-wide
or regional evacuations, the consequences of non-compliance are amplified because individual deviations propagate through the
network and affect thousands of other evacuees simultaneously. To this end, researchers have examined non-compliance behavior
and its contributing factors across diverse disaster contexts, as discussed in Section 3.1.
This subsection reviews the literature according to key evacuation decisions made by authorities. For each decision type, we
identify: (1) the directive issued, (2) observed public responses, (3) noncompliant actions taken as alternatives, and (4) contributing
behavioral factors, based on empirical research and predictive approaches. We also evaluate the extent to which each decision type
and disaster context has been addressed in prior studies. A summary of the findings is presented in Table 3.
A core decision in evacuation planning and management is the protective action directive, which determines whether people
should evacuate, shelter-in-place, or take no action, based on hazard characteristics, threat proximity, exposure risk, lead time, and
transportation conditions (Lindell et al., 2018; Lindell and Prater, 2007). Among these, evacuation has received the most scholarly
attention. Non-compliance with evacuation orders is influenced by a combination of logistical, sociodemographic, cognitive, and
situational factors. Traffic congestion remains one of the most cited deterrents, with 65% of non-evacuees during Hurricane Floyd
identifying it as the primary barrier (Dow and Cutter, 2000), while lack of transportation and financial constraints prevented many
from evacuating during Hurricane Katrina (Wolshon, 2002). More broadly, lack of vehicle access constitutes a fundamental barrier to
departure (Sarwar et al., 2018; Anyidoho et al., 2022; Sun et al., 2024). Sociodemographic characteristics further shape compliance:
age (Mileti et al., 1975; Perry, 1979a; Toledo et al., 2018), gender (Bateman and Edwards, 2002; Ling et al., 2021; Sun et al., 2024),
household composition (Carter et al., 1983; Gladwin and Peacock, 1997), disability (Van Willigen et al., 2002), income (Schaffer and
Cook, 1972; Bolin et al., 1996), length of residence (Gladwin and Peacock, 1997), pet ownership (Whitehead et al., 2000; Mozumder
et al., 2008; Solís et al., 2010; Yin et al., 2016), lower education level (Hasan et al., 2011; Sarwar et al., 2018), and larger household
size (Hasan et al., 2011). Distance to shelters and trust in authorities also play a role (Whitehead et al., 2000; Solís et al., 2010). Work
obligations during the evacuation period further constrain compliance (Hasan et al., 2011; Sarwar et al., 2018).
Homeownership and property attachment, including concern for belongings and livestock, can reduce willingness to leave (Hasan
et al., 2011; McLennan et al., 2019; Anyidoho et al., 2022; Elhami-Khorasani et al., 2023; Rashid et al., 2025), while confidence
in building safety, such as residing in a multi-floor home during flooding or having pre-installed protective measures, diminishes
the perceived need to evacuate (Hasan et al., 2012; Morooka and Yamada, 2017; Lim et al., 2019b; Nadimi et al., 2025). Warning
communication deficiencies constitute another critical barrier. Advisory orders are often perceived as non-urgent, resulting in delayed
responses or inaction, and the absence or ambiguity of official warnings further reduces compliance (Guan and Chen, 2021). Warning
