fatigue from previous false alarms erodes trust in future directives (Wolshon et al., 2005), while delayed warning delivery or very
short disaster arrival times leave insufficient opportunity to act (Syamsidik and Istiyanto, 2013).
Cognitive and perceptual barriers also play a significant role: low self-efficacy and overestimation of available evacuation time
lead individuals to misjudge their window for safe departure (Hall et al., 2017), while absence of prior evacuation experience and low
belief in the effectiveness of evacuation suppress protective action (Li et al., 2022b; Jin et al., 2025). Social dynamics further reinforce
non-compliance, as the influence of non-evacuating neighbors and the absence of social pressure from close contacts encourage
individuals to stay (Sadri et al., 2017a; Urata and Hato, 2021), particularly among those with weaker social networks and lower
perceived exposure (Sadri et al., 2017b). Situational conditions, including nighttime or cold-season occurrence (Nadimi et al., 2025)
and pandemic-related concerns such as fear of virus exposure at congregate shelters (Tsekeni et al., 2025), represent additional
barriers. Across all these dimensions, lack of perceived urgency or pressure to evacuate remains one of the most consistently reported
factors (An et al., 2015; Xu et al., 2016; Demiroluk et al., 2016; Urata and Pel, 2018; Hou et al., 2020; Wang et al., 2021; Chen et al.,
2021a; Bian et al., 2022b; Adjei et al., 2022; Diaz et al., 2023; Greer et al., 2023; Wong et al., 2023; Wu et al., 2024; Chen et al.,
2024; Ma and Lee, 2025; Elejalde et al., 2025).
Large-scale evacuations are not always viable and may cause life-threatening congestion if poorly managed (Bayram and Yaman,
2024). During Hurricane Rita (2005), 80% of 111 deaths were evacuation-related, with traffic spanning over 100 miles (Urbina and
Wolshon, 2003; Zachria and Patel, 2006). In such cases, a shelter-in-place directive may be issued (Sorensen et al., 2004). This
approach can be effective; in Australia, 75.7% of flash flood fatalities occurred outdoors versus 12% indoors (Haynes et al., 2009).
Non-compliance with shelter-in-place orders is tied to distrust, unsafe housing, or medical needs. Individuals with disabilities or
secure homes tend to stay, while others may evacuate despite advice (Whitehead et al., 2000; Golshani et al., 2019a; Ma and Lee,
2025). When perceived risk exceeds official assessment, individuals are more likely to self-evacuate regardless of shelter-in-place
directives (Wolshon et al., 2005; Elejalde et al., 2025), particularly those experiencing emotional distress and fear (Li et al., 2022b).
Structural vulnerability, such as residing in a mobile-home or being located close to the threat, further increases the likelihood of
departing against advice (Hasan et al., 2011; Demiroluk et al., 2016; Lim et al., 2019b). The desire to reunite with family members at
a different location is another significant motivator (Lambert et al., 2013), as is social influence from trusted contacts and alarming
messages spreading through large networks (Ling et al., 2021). Demographic and socioeconomic characteristics also matter: higher
income and socioeconomic status provide greater mobility and resources to self-evacuate (Hasan et al., 2011; Elejalde et al., 2025),
while being single, younger, or female is associated with a higher propensity to leave rather than shelter in place (Demiroluk et al.,
2016; Lim et al., 2019b).
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
15

S. Moslemi et al.
To enhance compliance, studies recommend early warnings, clear guidance, and public education (Ujihara, 1989; Jann, 1989;
McGee and Penning-Rowsell, 2022). In Australia, early decisions are encouraged to reduce uncertainty (Australasian Fire Authorities
Council, 2005; Handmer and Tibbits, 2005).
A take-no-action directive targets populations outside danger zones to reduce congestion and conserve emergency resources.
However, these can be disrupted by shadow evacuations, unauthorized departures by those not advised to evacuate, often driven
by unclear messaging or elevated risk perception. Such actions can strain transportation systems, as seen after the Three Mile Island
accident and Hurricane Ike (2008) (Lindell et al., 1983; Dueñas-Osorio et al., 2012; Ling et al., 2021; Ma and Lee, 2025). To mitigate
this, authorities should clearly define risk zones, communicate consistently, and address public concerns (Lindell and Prater, 2007).
While authorities can issue evacuation orders, they have limited control over individuals’ actual departure time (Beloglazov et al.,
2016). Although most evacuate after official warnings, some leave earlier, particularly those with prior evacuation experience (Jiang
et al., 2022; Makinoshima and Imamura, 2024; Ma and Lee, 2025), preparedness (e.g., identifying a safe destination) (Jiang et al.,
2022), clear guidance on actions (Gehlot et al., 2019a), or visual cues like smoke or flames, or deteriorating air-quality in nearby
areas (McLennan et al., 2013; Raei et al., 2025). Smartphone ownership, firsthand observation of the hazard, and familiarity with
community evacuation plans also facilitate earlier departure (Grajdura et al., 2021). Proximity to hazards increases perceived risk,
prompting earlier departure (Huang et al., 2012; Gehlot et al., 2019a; Guan and Chen, 2021; Elejalde et al., 2025), consistent with
findings linking threat severity to timing (Sorensen, 1991). Anticipated long travel times also encourage early evacuation to avoid
congestion (Hasan et al., 2013; Ng et al., 2015; Gehlot et al., 2019a; Golshani et al., 2020; Bian et al., 2023). Individuals living alone
may act sooner due to simplified decision-making, especially when messaging is clear (Gehlot et al., 2019a). Additionally, higher
education level, vehicle ownership, and greater financial preparedness are associated with earlier departure (Sarwar et al., 2018;
Ma and Lee, 2025), as are daytime hours (Anyidoho et al., 2022) and participation in public preparedness programs that increase
risk-awareness (Wambura and Wong, 2025). Concern about potential danger, delay, or rerouting can also encourage some evacuees
to leave before traffic management measures take effect (Bian et al., 2022b).
Many still delay evacuation after receiving official warnings due to cognitive processing and pre-evacuation tasks such as gathering
information, contacting family, retrieving children, collecting essentials, or making intermediate trips (Murray-Tuite and Mahmassani,
2003; Sorensen et al., 2004; Murray-Tuite and Mahmassani, 2004; Bernardini et al., 2016; Toledo et al., 2018; Wang et al., 2021;
Chen et al., 2022; Elhami-Khorasani et al., 2023; Makinoshima and Imamura, 2024; Reyes-Norambuena et al., 2025). Mothers,
unemployed parents, and affluent households are more likely to retrieve children (Zimmerman et al., 2007; Chaudhari et al., 2010;
Liu et al., 2012, 2014b), while commuters often return home to secure possessions (Chaudhari et al., 2010). These actions contribute
to delayed departures and congestion. For instance, during the 2011 Tohoku Earthquake and Tsunami, over 80% of residents delayed
evacuation for such reasons, increasing fatalities (Chiu and Mirchandani, 2008; Haynes et al., 2010; Yun and Hamada, 2012; Suppasri
et al., 2013; Lindell et al., 2018).
Departure time remains highly variable and difficult to predict due to diverse pre-evacuation activities. To address this, models
have estimated departure time distributions using S-curves (Radwan et al., 1985; Kalafatas and Peeta, 2009; Xie et al., 2010; Li et al.,
2013; Woo et al., 2017), logistic curves (Southworth, 1991), Rayleigh (Tweedie et al., 1986), exponential (Hobeika and Kim, 1998),
and Poisson-based methods (Cova and Johnson, 2003). More recent approaches apply regression, neural networks, survival analysis
(Wilmot and Mei, 2004; Fu et al., 2007; Pham et al., 2020; Grajdura et al., 2021), random parameter probit models (Sadri et al.,
2013), hazard-based models (Hasan et al., 2013; Gehlot et al., 2019a), and heuristic algorithms (Zhao et al., 2022).
These models predict departure timing using a set of predefined influential factors. Preparatory activities, such as gathering information, notifying family, collecting essentials, and making intermediate trips, consume time before departure begins. Psychological
and cognitive barriers further contribute to delay: stress, unfamiliarity with hazards, limited reliable information, and reliance on
traditional media slow decision-making (Wilmot and Mei, 2004; Fu et al., 2007; Pham et al., 2020; Grajdura et al., 2021). Low-risk
recognition is a particularly strong predictor, with evacuees who do not perceive themselves at risk departing significantly later (An
et al., 2015; Urata and Pel, 2018; Roy and Hasan, 2021; Iwai, 2026), and overestimation of the evacuation window compounds
this effect (Wolshon et al., 2005; Hall et al., 2017). Insufficient trust in official warnings (Jin et al., 2025), unclear or insufficiently
detailed warning messages (McLennan et al., 2019), and uncertainty about storm track and intensity forecasts, including hope that
evacuation may become unnecessary, contribute to hesitation (Wolshon et al., 2005; Hall et al., 2017), while insufficient pressure
from officials further reduces the urgency to act (Wong et al., 2020a). Waiting to observe others’ decisions before acting also delays
individual departure (An et al., 2015). sociodemographic and household characteristics shape departure timing as well: older adults,
individuals with disabilities, and less-educated households tend to evacuate later (Gehlot et al., 2019a; Golshani et al., 2019b; Elejalde
et al., 2025), as do those with lower income and long-term residence (Grajdura et al., 2021). Male gender is also associated with
later departure (Grajdura et al., 2021; Anyidoho et al., 2022). Presence of children, large household size, and absence of a mandatory
evacuation order further delay departure (Dixit et al., 2012; Sarwar et al., 2018; Golshani et al., 2020; Rambha et al., 2021), while
lack of vehicle access (Wambura and Wong, 2025) and owning multiple vehicles, which adds coordination difficulties, prolong the
process (Rambha et al., 2021).
Evidence on insurance coverage is mixed and appears to be context-dependent. In a hurricane setting, insurance coverage has
been associated with later departure, which the authors attribute to a reduced perception of financial risk from hurricane damage
(Jiang et al., 2022). In a wildfire stated-preference study, by contrast, full homeowners’ or renters’ coverage was associated with
earlier intended departure (Ma and Lee, 2025).
Situational and environmental conditions present additional barriers: residence outside high-risk areas (Guan and Chen, 2021),
expectation of reduced hazard severity or possible change in hurricane direction (Sarwar et al., 2018), and preference for daytime
evacuation (Sarwar et al., 2018) reduce urgency, while low visibility due to smoke or nighttime conditions and evening or nighttime
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
16

S. Moslemi et al.
hours more broadly cause hesitation (Wong et al., 2020a; Grajdura et al., 2021; Anyidoho et al., 2022). Perceived traffic congestion
and uncertainty about the safety of escape routes and effectiveness of traffic management measures may further discourage timely
departure (Wong et al., 2020a; Bian et al., 2022b; Grajdura and Rowangould, 2025). Destination-related characteristics also influence
timing: individuals staying with friends, using commercial accommodations, or traveling shorter distances often perceive less urgency
and delay departure (Hasan et al., 2013; Ng et al., 2015; Golshani et al., 2019a).
Authorities not only issue evacuation orders but may also recommend transportation modes based on disaster type, safety
concerns, evacuee location, travel distance, and commuting patterns (Murray-Tuite and Wolshon, 2013; Giovanna et al., 2016; Liu
et al., 2014a). For instance, rapid-onset events like tsunamis limit mode-flexibility (Iwai, 2026), while slower-developing disasters
such as hurricanes allow more considered choices. Long evacuation distances require reliable transport, whereas shorter ones permit
alternatives like walking. Authorities also consider likely evacuee locations, such as home, work, or elsewhere, and their commuting
behavior to assess feasible transportation modes (Murray-Tuite and Wolshon, 2013).
Transportation mode selection among evacuees is shaped not only by official guidance but also by personal preferences, perceived
risk, and constraints, especially access to private vehicles. Evacuees with vehicles often follow recommendations due to flexibility,
space for belongings and family, and access to real-time information, such as radio updates (Liu et al., 2014a; An et al., 2015; Lindell
et al., 2015; Togawa et al., 2018; Golshani et al., 2020; Wong et al., 2020a). Some use multiple vehicles to preserve assets, while
others consolidate to minimize logistical complications and stay together (Murray-Tuite et al., 2012; Elhami-Khorasani et al., 2023).
The perceived speed, convenience, and independence offered by private vehicles further reinforce the preference for personal cars
over public alternatives, particularly when car-ownership enables faster and more autonomous departure (Jin et al., 2025). However,
not all comply with these recommendations; some avoid driving due to concerns over severe traffic congestion. During Hurricane
Floyd (1999), travel times increased up to tenfold due to storm intensity, its coastal-parallel path, high evacuee volume, and strained
infrastructure (Dow and Cutter, 2002). Similarly, during Hurricane Rita (2005), fuel shortages and secondary accidents led to a
160-kilometer traffic jam (Patrick, 2005).
Those without private vehicles, including low-income individuals, older adults, people with disabilities, and others with mobility
or health limitations, rely on carpooling, public transit, or walking (Deka and Carnegie, 2010; Renne et al., 2011). Carpooling is more
common among unmarried evacuees, older adults, and financially-constrained individuals (Wu et al., 2012; Sadri et al., 2014b).
Public transit is vital for vulnerable groups, particularly the elderly and disabled (Bian and Wilmot, 2018; Wambura and Wong,
2024), though its usefulness depends on destination type and service design. Sadri et al. (2014b) found evacuees heading to hotels
used buses, while those staying with relatives often avoided evacuation buses due to baggage and accessibility issues. Yet fixedroutes, crowding, and limited service reduce transit effectiveness during emergencies (Wolshon et al., 2005). Several factors also
drive evacuees’ preference for personal vehicles over public transit, including perceived comfort and shorter waiting time, the desire
to protect vehicular property, the need to make intermediate stops for family gathering or supplies, and the ability to reach farther
destinations (Wolshon et al., 2005; Sadri et al., 2014a; An et al., 2015; Troncoso Parady and Hato, 2016; Bian et al., 2019; Golshani
et al., 2020). Limited public-transit service in the area and the presence of elderly household members further discourage bus use
(Sadri et al., 2014a; Bian et al., 2019). Where neither private nor public options are available, walking may be the only alternative,
though it poses risks such as hazard exposure, crowding, and poor infrastructure, especially in dense urban areas (Mas et al., 2020).
Demographic characteristics also shape the propensity to walk: female evacuees, older adults, and those with lower education are
more likely to evacuate on foot, as are residents in high-density areas with nearby accessible destinations (An et al., 2015; Bian et al.,
2019).
As noted by Abdelgawad and Abdulhai (2009), evacuation destination choice is a key aspect of effective evacuation planning.
Destination attributes, such as safety, comfort, medical care, food provision, and communication, significantly influence decisions
(Charnkol et al., 2007). Safety concerns vary by hazard: tsunami evacuees often prioritize inland or vertical evacuation (Harnantyari
et al., 2020), and many avoid destinations perceived as closer to danger, highlighting the role of geographic proximity in planning
(Yamada and Yamasaki, 2021; Wang and Wallace, 2022). Perceptual and institutional factors also shape destination decisions. Some
evacuees regard distant locations as safer, despite increased travel risk (Lindell and Perry, 2012), while others hold misconceptions,
such as the belief that moving inland during a tsunami is sufficient without gaining adequate vertical height (Hall et al., 2017). Distrust
in local shelters has been linked to non-compliance (Lambert et al., 2013; McCaughey et al., 2017; Wong et al., 2020a). Individual
differences in threat appraisal, coping appraisal, past experience, and personal characteristics also shape which destination evacuees
ultimately select (Mahittikul et al., 2024).
Although public shelters are officially designated, studies consistently show evacuees often prefer alternatives. Across multiple
hurricanes, 44–70% stayed with friends or relatives, 7–46% used hotels, and only 2–11% relied on public shelters (Prater et al., 2000;
Whitehead, 2003; Smith and McCarty, 2009; Cheng et al., 2011; Lindell et al., 2011; Wu et al., 2012; Wilmot et al., 2013; Wu et al.,
2013; Yin et al., 2014a; Wong et al., 2018).
Researchers employ discrete choice models, including multinomial logit, nested logit, cross-nested logit, mixed logit, and probit
formulations (Sheffi, 1985a), to predict evacuation destination decisions based on sociodemographic characteristics, destination attributes, and perceptual or institutional influences. According to these studies, staying with friends or relatives is associated with
flexibility, informal arrangements, and shared costs, especially among non-white evacuees, pet owners, older adults, and individuals
with higher education (Whitehead et al., 2000; Wong, 2020; Damera et al., 2020; Bian et al., 2023). Social connections, prior familiarity with the destination, and longer residential stability further reinforce this preference, as evacuees tend to choose locations
they have previously visited or where strong interpersonal ties exist (An et al., 2015; Jiang et al., 2021; Bian et al., 2019; Ma and
Lee, 2025; Toledo et al., 2018). The desire for comfort, privacy, and the ability to bring pets and personal belongings to private locations also drives evacuees away from public shelters, with households with children particularly favoring private accommodations
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
17

S. Moslemi et al.
(Wolshon et al., 2005; Jin et al., 2025; Grajdura and Rowangould, 2025). Hotels are typically selected by evacuees with greater
financial resources, with choices shaped by income, gender, household size, distance, and pet ownership, while delayed departure
reduces hotel use (Whitehead et al., 2000; Mesa-Arango et al., 2013; Yin et al., 2014a; Lim et al., 2016; Bian et al., 2019). Public shelters are more often used by ethnic minorities, low-income groups, and late evacuees with limited options, whereas younger
adults, white households, and residents near hazard-zones are less likely to use them (Mileti et al., 1975; Whitehead et al., 2000;
Baker, 2000; Wu et al., 2012; Mesa-Arango et al., 2013; Wong et al., 2020b). Under panic, confusion, or unclear danger conditions,
evacuees may avoid designated shelters in favor of nearby open spaces perceived as wide, less damaged, and familiar (Bernardini
et al., 2016; Chen and Cheng, 2020), while lack of awareness about shelter locations and insufficient public education further reduce
their utilization (Muthohar et al., 2020). Health- and pandemic-related concerns, such as fear of disease transmission in congregate
shelters, have also reduced willingness to use public facilities (Chen et al., 2024; Tsekeni et al., 2025), while insufficient capacity and
physical obstacles at designated areas can render them less suitable (D’Amico et al., 2024). Network congestion may further reduce
the accessibility of assigned shelters (Cheng et al., 2011), and vehicle ownership enables evacuees to reach more distant alternatives,
including private accommodations with family or friends (Troncoso Parady and Hato, 2016). Mobility constraints also lead disabled
and carless evacuees to prefer nearby familiar destinations over more distant public shelters (Wambura and Wong, 2025).
Taken together, these findings emphasize that public shelters, while central to emergency planning, are frequently underutilized.
Their use increases under authoritative directives (Lim et al., 2016) and among less-prepared households (Golshani et al., 2019a),
highlighting the need for more adaptive and targeted sheltering strategies that account for behavioral heterogeneity, accessibility
constraints, and evolving public health considerations.
In addition to recommending destinations, authorities often provide evacuation routes to manage traffic flow. Routing strategies
generally fall into four categories: system optimal (SO), user equilibrium (UE), nearest allocation, and constrained system optimal
(CSO). SO minimizes total travel time, UE assumes self-optimization with perfect information, NA assigns evacuees to the nearest safe
location, and CSO emphasizes fairness by limiting route-length disparities (Sheffi, 1985b; Bayram et al., 2015; Bayram, 2016; Bayram
and Yaman, 2018a; Bayram et al., 2023). However, evacuees frequently deviate from assigned routes, favoring personal judgment
over official instructions (Wu et al., 2012). Such behavior reflects bounded rationality and incomplete or evolving information, often
leading to misjudgments in travel time and congestion, thereby reducing overall system efficiency (Simon, 1957, 1972; Tong and
Feiler, 2017; Bayram, 2024). This finding shows the need to incorporate pedestrian dynamics into evacuation planning, as their
exclusion can lead to unrealistic models, underestimated travel times, and misaligned-response strategies.
Route choice is typically modeled using pre-trip, en route, or hybrid approaches. Pre-trip models assume fixed decisions made
before departure (Florian et al., 2008), en-route models allow for real-time adjustments based on congestion or closures (Pel et al.,
2009), and hybrid models combine initial planning with mid-trip adaptation (Prato, 2009).
Familiarity significantly influences route selection, with evacuees often preferring known routes over official ones, contributing
to localized congestion (Deka and Carnegie, 2010; Lindell et al., 2011; Wu et al., 2012; Intini et al., 2019; Chen and Cheng, 2020;
Wang et al., 2021; Bian et al., 2022c; Lessan and Kim, 2022; Elhami-Khorasani et al., 2023; Wambura and Wong, 2025; Mapa et al.,
2026). Past experience also plays a key role: during Hurricane Bret (1999), 69% of evacuees relied on experience, while only 3%
followed official routes (Prater et al., 2000). Similarly, Yang et al. (1993) found experienced drivers were less likely to seek external
guidance. Network characteristics, such as link usage, route overlap, and real-time congestion, and sociodemographic factors like age
also affect route decisions (Chiu and Mirchandani, 2008; Dulebenets et al., 2019; Lim et al., 2019a; Xu et al., 2022b).
As en-route and hybrid models suggest, evacuees adapt routes in response to environmental changes or access to critical services
(Arentze and Timmermans, 2005; Akbarzadeh and Wilmot, 2015). During dynamic events like floods or no-notice evacuations,
perceived safety, road conditions, and official recommendations shape decisions (Chiu and Mirchandani, 2008; Hsu and Peeta, 2013;
Wang et al., 2020). Environmental and physical conditions along designated routes, such as proximity to active hazards, debris, and
blocked streets, can compel evacuees to deviate toward perceived safer alternatives (Bernardini et al., 2016; D’Amico et al., 2024;
Raei et al., 2025). These challenges have led to the rise of intelligent transportation systems (ITS), using IoT, GPS, sensors, and
connected platforms to monitor traffic and optimize routing. Machine learning and optimization models support dynamic strategies
to reduce congestion, yet evacuees often prioritize personal over system-wide efficiency (Lambert et al., 2013). Hence, equitable
routing models aligning individual and collective interests are crucial. While real-time traffic data can increase compliance (Bonsall
and Palmer, 1999; Adler, 2001; Robinson and Khattak, 2010; Huang et al., 2017), effectiveness depends on information credibility.
In Texas, 9%–37% of households ignored official routes due to distrust in authorities, safety concerns, or peer influence (Lindell et al.,
2001; De Maio, 2013; Muthohar et al., 2020). The growing use of real-time navigation tools may further facilitate deviation from
official routes by dynamically directing evacuees toward alternatives that do not necessarily align with coordinated evacuation plans
(Wong et al., 2020a). Advances in connected and autonomous vehicles offer potential for more coordinated and fair evacuations
(Bayram, 2024). Individual risk tolerance also shapes route decisions, as risk-seeking evacuees are more inclined to rely on personal
judgment and self-selected routes rather than following official guidance (Ma and Lee, 2025).
Household interaction also affects route decisions. Families typically act as interdependent units, and emotional contagion, driven
by panic or fear, can reduce compliance and lead to suboptimal decisions (Leach, 1994; Hatfield, 1994; Lickel et al., 2000; Sherman
et al., 2002). Sadri et al. (2014b) used a mixed logit model to study intra-household coordination during Hurricane Ivan in householddecision-making. More recently, Sheu (2024) proposed a framework accounting for cognitive, emotional, and social influences.
Psychological strain during evacuation also impacts in-vehicle behavior. Studies report that stress correlates with hazardous
driving, such as sudden acceleration, frequent lane changes, and traffic violations, raising crash risks and disrupting flow (Abdelgawad
and Abdulhai, 2009; Tu et al., 2010). Infrastructure features like road width, lane count, and headway spacing interact with such
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
18

S. Moslemi et al.
behaviors, affecting throughput (Dulebenets et al., 2019). Route-switching due to congestion, common in large-scale events, can
further destabilize networks (Knoop et al., 2010), reinforcing the need for behaviorally realistic traffic models.
While most studies focus on vehicles, pedestrian route choice remains underexplored. Early research found pedestrians favor
the shortest path (Seneviratne and Morrall, 1985), while more recent work highlights the effects of route width, density, walking
capacity, and distance (Han et al., 2017; Troncoso Parady and Hato, 2016). Poor or unclear evacuation signage and unfamiliarity
with designated routes can cause confusion, leading evacuees to self-navigate toward alternative locations (Chen et al., 2022), while
physical damage, fear of adjacent buildings, and crowd influence may further shift pedestrian route choices (Bernardini et al., 2016).
Moreover, walking speed declines with age, physical condition, and crowd pressure; at densities above 3.0 persons/m2
, speeds can fall
below 0.2 m/s (Older, 1968; Moussaïd et al., 2010). This finding shows the need to incorporate pedestrian dynamics into evacuation
planning, as their exclusion can lead to unrealistic models, underestimated travel times, and misaligned response strategies.
The coexistence of pedestrians and vehicles during evacuations inherently results in mixed traffic conditions. Mixed traffic at
intersections intensifies congestion and reduces road capacity. This issue is further amplified during evacuation, where common
mixed-traffic control strategies, such as traffic signals, often fail as evacuees prioritize escape. While authorities provide routing
recommendations, they typically do not assign specific road segments to vehicles and pedestrians, leaving mixed traffic unmanaged.
Mixed traffic evacuation, which coordinates multiple transportation modes to optimize capacity and efficiency, aims to address these
challenges (Abdelgawad et al., 2010). While various models have been developed to manage these interactions, only Li et al. (2019a)
specifically explored the use of barriers to separate road segments for pedestrians and vehicles and determine their optimal locations.
Using physical barriers is a practical way to enhance compliance, particularly when adherence to traffic signals is low. However, its key
weaknesses are that it only partially controls mixed traffic instead of being fully managed at all entry directions of intersections and
restricts vehicle movement despite already limited road capacity. Since vehicle capacity is more constrained than pedestrian capacity,
further restricting vehicles can worsen evacuation delays. As a result, mixed traffic still exists at intersections, requiring local traffic
management. Since such models have not yet been explored, evacuee non-compliance, whereby individuals ignore designated road
segments, also remains unexamined. Future research should focus on fully separating road segments for pedestrians or vehicles at all
entry of intersections and assessing evacuee compliance with these restrictions.
Our review reveals that the majority of existing studies on large-scale evacuations emphasize four primary evacuation decision
areas : route selection, departure timing, destination choice, and protective actions. In contrast, transportation mode has received
comparatively less attention, and road-segment designation was rarely addressed, highlighting critical gaps in the literature.
Fig. 4 provides a detailed view of how papers were classified within each key evacuation decision, helping to reveal the distribution and thematic emphasis of existing research. Within the protective action category, most studies prioritize evacuation behavior
(53 papers); shelter-in-place strategies have received less attention (21 papers), and shadow evacuations remain almost entirely unexplored (five papers). Furthermore, research on departure time primarily focuses on identifying effective influencing factors (35
papers), followed by departure components (19 papers) and estimation models (17 papers), while authorities’ criteria remain the
least explored area (two papers). In destination selection, studies give the most attention to public shelters (16 papers), followed by
general effective factors (12 papers) and friends’ homes (12 papers), with commercial facilities receiving the least focus (five papers).
Moreover, route selection research predominantly explores en-route models (31 papers), which allow evacuees to adjust their routes
dynamically, compared to general information (10 papers) and pre-trip models that assume a fixed route choice before departure.
Building on these findings, the behavioral patterns and contributing factors underlying noncompliance with critical evacuation
decisions have led to varying assumptions in evacuation optimization models. Section 4 examines how these models represent compliance behavior across the key decision types discussed.
4. Optimization-based modeling and solution approaches integrating evacuee behavior considerations
Two comprehensive surveys (Caunhye et al., 2012; Bayram, 2016) emphasize the significance of integrating human behavior
into evacuation optimization models. Since large-scale urban and regional evacuations often involve hundreds of thousands of individuals traversing extensive road networks, optimization models must balance computational tractability with behaviorally realistic
representations of compliance. However, many existing evacuation models still fail to fully capture key behavioral factors, despite
growing evidence that integrating such considerations can substantially improve the realism and effectiveness of evacuation planning (Aldahlawi et al., 2024). Based on the reviewed literature, evacuation optimization models account for compliance behavior in
two main approaches: some explicitly incorporate compliance behavior into decision-making, while others focus on the underlying
determinants of compliance, such as socio–demographic attributes, personal abilities, psychological traits, and structural plan/design
features. Sections 4.1 and 4.2 provide a detailed discussion of these two perspectives.
4.1. Direct integration of evacuee compliance in optimization models
Optimization models adopting the first approach explicitly embed compliance behavior within their decision-making frameworks.
Table 4 summarizes these studies, highlighting their optimization approaches, disaster types, evacuation-related decisions influenced
by compliance considerations, compliance degree (full, partial, or non-compliance), and key factors affecting evacuees’ compliance
levels.

Methodology: SO = system optimal; CSO = constrained system optimal; UE = user equilibrium; SUE = stochastic user equilibrium;
DUE = dynamic user equilibrium; ESUE = extended stochastic user equilibrium; BRUE = boundedly rational user equilibrium; ISE
= integrated simulation and optimization; PSL = path-size logit.
Evacuation optimization models often involve critical decisions, where behavioral assumptions significantly influence model structure. One commonly adopted approach is the system-optimal (SO) routing model, which reflects a centralized planning perspective
aimed at minimizing total evacuation time and reducing congestion, under the assumption of full evacuee compliance, where individuals are assumed to strictly follow assigned routes (Wardrop and Whitehead, 1952; Sheffi, 1985a; Bayram, 2016). Although
SO models are primarily defined by routing, this compliance typically extends to all evacuation-related decisions, with most models
assuming universal adherence.
As shown in Table 4, System-optimal models are classified by disaster context, optimization approach, and influenced decisions.
While many address general evacuation scenarios, some focus on specific contexts such as hurricanes (e.g., Lim et al., 2012; Bish
et al., 2014), tsunamis (Li et al., 2019b), urban fires (Alçada-Almeida et al., 2009; Coutinho-Rodrigues et al., 2012), and nuclear,
biological, and chemical (NBC) hazards (Bayram and Yaman, 2024).
The dominant methodology is mixed-integer linear programming (MILP), frequently combined with the cell transmission model
(CTM) or dynamic network flow frameworks (e.g., Bretschneider and Kimms, 2012; Hamacher et al., 2013; Pillac et al., 2016; He
et al., 2018), alongside linear programming (Zheng and Chiu, 2011; Kimms and Maiwald, 2017) and nonlinear formulations (Han
et al., 2006). Structural extensions include bi-level programming (Liu et al., 2025) and multi-objective models (Alçada-Almeida et al.,
2009; Coutinho-Rodrigues et al., 2012; Goerigk et al., 2014; Kimms and Maiwald, 2018).
All models incorporate route assignment; however, many also optimize shelter assignment (e.g., Lim et al., 2012; Bretschneider and Kimms, 2012; He et al., 2015; Pillac et al., 2016), departure timing and staging (Bish et al., 2014; Tuydes-Yaman and
Ziliaskopoulos, 2014; He et al., 2015; Kimms and Maiwald, 2017), contraflow operation (Pillac et al., 2016; Kimms and Maiwald,
2017; He et al., 2018; Kimms and Maiwald, 2018), crossing-elimination (Bretschneider and Kimms, 2011, 2012), and shelter location
(Alçada-Almeida et al., 2009; Coutinho-Rodrigues et al., 2012; Goerigk et al., 2014). A few studies also consider evacuate-or-shelterTransportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611

in-place decisions, dynamic resource allocation (Bayram and Yaman, 2024), and traffic signal timing (Liu et al., 2025). Although SO
models are theoretically tractable and serve as useful benchmarks for large-scale network optimization, their real-world applicability
is limited due to the fact that full compliance is rarely observed in mass evacuations.
To better represent individual-level autonomy, many models adopt the user-equilibrium (UE) paradigm. In this framework, evacuees are assumed to minimize their individual travel times based on perceived conditions, leading to decentralized yet equilibrium
outcomes (Wardrop and Whitehead, 1952; Sheffi, 1985a). From a behavioral standpoint, this reflects a different form of compliance,
whereby evacuees follow their own preferences rather than official directives.
Several studies apply UE principles through bi-level modeling. Shen et al. (2008) and Kulshrestha et al. (2011) formulate models
in which upper-level decisions, such as shelter location or assignment, are optimized, while evacuees independently select routes at
the lower-level based on user equilibrium. Gu et al. (2025), Ng et al. (2010), and Zhang et al. (2024c) also employ bi-level models
with deterministic UE routing at the lower-level, incorporating the BPR function to represent traffic congestion. While Ng et al. (2010)
address a general evacuation scenario with shelter assignment modeled at the upper-level, Gu et al. (2025) focus on urban flooding,
assigning origin zones to pre-designated evacuation exits. In contrast, Zhang et al. (2024c) propose a two-phase upper-level approach
for earthquake evacuation: the first phase minimizes the number of new shelters, and the second minimizes total evacuation time by
optimizing shelter location and assignment decisions.
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
23

S. Moslemi et al.
Fig. 5. Number of partial-compliance optimization studies by 5-year publication period (𝑛 = 9).
Similarly, Xie et al. (2010) and Karoonsoontawong and Lin (2011) apply deterministic UE to model route choice at the lowerlevel and incorporate upper-level strategies involving lane reversal. Both models simulate time-dependent traffic dynamics using
the CTM within a deterministic bi-level optimization framework. Extending these foundations, dynamic UE has been increasingly
incorporated into evacuation models to better capture evacuee decision-making under evolving hazard and network conditions.
Apivatanagul et al. (2012) and Li et al. (2011) introduced early stochastic bi-level frameworks optimizing evacuation, shelter-in-place,
and shelter location strategies, with evacuees dynamically selecting routes based on changing travel conditions. Li et al. (2012) further
advanced stochastic bi-level evacuation modeling by developing a shelter location model that integrates evacuee dynamic route choice
across multiple hurricane scenarios. Extending this line of research, Davidson et al. (2020) introduced the integrated scenario-based
evacuation (ISE) model, integrating multiple hazards and building on the earlier formulations of Apivatanagul et al. (2012) and Li
et al. (2011). Yang et al. (2019a) and Yang et al. (2019b) operationalized the ISE framework in real-world hurricane case studies,
emphasizing adaptive and repeated evacuation strategies. More recently, Afkham et al. (2022) applied a bi-level dynamic UE model
to wildfire evacuation, modeling self-evacuee route choice combined with infrastructure allocation under fire-spread uncertainty.
Several studies propose behaviorally enriched extensions of classical UE models. Xie and Turnquist (2009) and Liu and Luo
(2012) both employ deterministic bi-level models with stochastic user equilibrium (SUE) at the lower-level to represent evacuee route
choice under uncertainty. In Xie and Turnquist (2009), stochasticity arises through probabilistic route selection to support integrated
planning of shelter allocation and lane control. Liu and Luo (2012) further enhance behavioral realism by applying random utility
theory to model evacuees’ perceived travel costs and probabilistic route decisions, thereby accounting for perception errors and
bounded-rationality. Xu and Nair (2024), Xu et al. (2024) present an expanded stochastic user equilibrium (ESUE) framework that
integrates random regret minimization (RRM) with bounded rational user equilibrium (BRUE), and evaluate it alongside other models
including deterministic UE, SUE, BRUE-1, BRUE-2, and a non-equilibrium baseline. Their findings show that behaviorally realistic
models, particularly SUE, BRUE, and ESUE, achieve lower evacuation time, congestion, and infrastructure costs, thereby enhancing
robustness and realism in evacuation modeling.
These models often rely on optimistic assumptions about awareness, rationality, and access to information. The challenge lies in
designing evacuation plans that guide behavior under such imperfect conditions.
To address these challenges, researchers have developed constrained system-optimal (CSO) models, which relax the assumption of
full compliance by incorporating fairness-based constraints. Bayram et al. (2015) and Bayram and Yaman (2018a) introduce models
that restrict route assignments to paths whose lengths do not exceed a specified threshold relative to the shortest available route.
Compliance is assumed if this condition is satisfied. These models focus primarily on distance-based fairness and operate under known
compliance behavior. Bayram and Yaman (2018b) extend this framework to a stochastic setting for earthquake evacuation in Istanbul,
simultaneously incorporating uncertainty in demand, road capacity degradation, and shelter disruption, while preserving the CSO
tolerance constraint to ensure route acceptability across scenarios.
Yan et al. (2018) extend the CSO framework by incorporating social fairness through location-based risk prioritization. Their
model, based on the CTM, integrates dynamic traffic assignment and evacuation scheduling with centrally assigned routes and no
individual decision-making. A weighted objective function enables planners to balance fairness and efficiency by prioritizing populations in higher-risk areas. Although compliance is not behaviorally modeled, it is implicitly addressed through the assignment of
acceptable, priority-based evacuation plans, making the model a fairness-aware extension of the CSO framework. Similarly, Yuan
et al. (2019) extend the CSO framework by introducing residence-time constraints to regulate departure timing at the residential
level. Full compliance is assumed, conditional on residence times remaining within a predefined tolerance, reflecting the expectation
that evacuees will follow assigned schedules if delays are perceived as fair. By ensuring temporal equity across zones and avoiding
excessive delays in low-risk areas, the model improves the acceptability of authority-directed evacuation plans.
While constrained system-optimal (CSO) models introduce fairness-based constraints, they still assume full compliance when limited
conditions are satisfied, offering only a partial reflection of real-world evacuee behavior. To better capture varying levels of adherence,
recent studies have proposed models that explicitly incorporate partial compliance. Fig. 5 shows the publication timeline of the nine
partial-compliance studies identified in Table 4: no such model was published before 2014, with one appearing in 2011–2015 (Hsu
and Peeta, 2014), three in 2016–2020 (Zeng et al., 2016; Sun et al., 2017; Yi et al., 2017), and five in 2021–2026 (Wang and Wallace,
2022; Lessan and Kim, 2022; Xu and Nair, 2024; Xu et al., 2025).
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
24

S. Moslemi et al.
Among these, Lessan and Kim (2022) addresses behavioral uncertainty in short- and no-notice events such as wildfires. The
authors introduce a diversion matrix to represent deviations from assigned routes and evaluate system performance across four
cases. Case I assumes full compliance. Case II optimizes under full compliance but evaluates performance under observed deviations.
Case III integrates a fixed, known diversion matrix into the optimization. Case IV captures uncertain compliance by generating
multiple diversion matrices from a uniform distribution and solving a two-stage stochastic nonlinear program using sample average
approximation (SAA) and conditional value-at-risk (CVaR). In all cases, the objective is to minimize total evacuation time. Arc travel
times are modeled using the Bureau of Public Roads (BPR) function:

both the absence of guidance and the direct mapping of system-optimal proportions to linguistic messages without accounting for
behavioral responses.
However, beyond routing, destination assignment also presents challenges when evacuee behavior is not explicitly modeled (Abdelgawad and Abdulhai, 2010). Wang and Wallace (2022) propose a two-stage stochastic optimization model for assigning each
demand point 𝑖 to a single pick-up point 𝑗, accounting for partial compliance. The first stage determines assignments, while the second stage evaluates scenario-based compliance realizations 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆, with 𝜙𝑖𝑠 ∈ [0, 1] representing the proportion of compliant evacuees.

time, with constraints for assignment, capacity, and compliance. Results show the BETTER strategy enhances robustness by enabling
flexible redistribution and reducing both overflow and delay. Xu et al. (2025) extend this line of research to flood evacuation by jointly
optimizing shelter assignment and shelter-in-place site deployment under stochastic non-compliance within a two-stage stochastic
programming framework. In this context, shelter-in-place refers not to staying at home but to pre-deployed nearby facilities such as
high-rise buildings or elevated platforms that provide immediate local refuge without long-distance evacuation. Unlike Wang and
Wallace (2022), who optimize pick-up point assignment without considering shelter-in-place, Xu et al. (2025) integrate shelter-inplace as an alternative strategy that mitigates non-compliance by reducing the number of evacuees directed to distant shelters. Before
disasters, shelter-in-place sites of varying capacity are deployed at selected demand points; after disasters, evacuees are guided either
to a nearby site or to a remote shelter, and non-compliant evacuees redirect toward more attractive alternatives. Non-compliance is
modeled using the distance-and-direction-based attractiveness formulation of Wang and Wallace (2022) (Eq. (9)), extended to capture
heterogeneous behavior across multiple evacuee types through type-specific sensitivity parameters. A dam-break flooding case study
demonstrates that accounting for stochastic non-compliance leads to spatially decentralized shelter-in-place deployment, reducing
the negative impacts of behavioral uncertainty on shelter overflow and evacuation costs.
Another essential aspect of evacuation planning is compliance with evacuation orders, commonly referred to as the participation
rate, which is central to Sun et al. (2017). They develop a dynamic optimization model that improves hurricane evacuation by
delivering targeted-warnings through multiple channels, including official authorities, the National Weather Service (NWS), media,
and social networks. Evacuees are categorized into two groups: those with access to private vehicles, who can flexibly determine
their departure time and destination, and those reliant on public transit, who face capacity and scheduling-constraints requiring
centralized-coordination.
The model seeks to maximize total evacuation participation over a multi-period horizon by incorporating time- and sourcedependent compliance behavior. A key parameter, 𝑝𝑖𝑗𝑡, denotes the proportion of evacuees of type 𝑖 who respond to a warning from
source 𝑗 at time 𝑡. These response rates are based on behavioral research and expert input and vary according to timing, source
credibility, and evacuee characteristics such as vehicle ownership and household structure. The response rate is integrated through
the binary decision variable 𝑥𝑖𝑗𝑘𝑡, defined as 1 if a warning is issued to evacuees of type 𝑖 in zone 𝑘 from source 𝑗 at time 𝑡, and 0
otherwise. The number of evacuees who respond is modeled as 𝑥𝑖𝑗𝑘𝑡 ⋅ 𝑝𝑖𝑗𝑡 ⋅ 𝑛𝑖𝑘𝑡, where 𝑛𝑖𝑘𝑡 is the population of that group in that zone
and time, dynamically updated based on prior departures. This dependency introduces nonlinearity into the objective function.
To solve the model, the authors implement a greedy time-step heuristic: warning decisions are optimized period by period, the
population is updated accordingly, and the process repeats. This sequential method efficiently produces near-optimal results while
accounting for evolving compliance patterns.
Unlike earlier studies that model compliance with a single evacuation decision—such as routing, destination selection, or
participation—Yi et al. (2017) proposes a more comprehensive framework that integrates multiple evacuation decisions. Their bi-level

stochastic optimization model simultaneously considers route choice, destination selection, and evacuation timing under hurricane
uncertainty. Route choice is modeled using dynamic user equilibrium, capturing noncompliant, self-routing behavior. In contrast,
compliance with assigned evacuation timing and destinations is explicitly modeled using discrete choice approaches. Behavioral
variation is represented through scenarios 𝑠 ∈ 𝑆, each reflecting different hurricane paths and timings.
Evacuation timing is estimated using a sequential binary logit model, following Fu and Wilmot (2004). For each origin 𝑧, time
period 𝑡, and scenario 𝑠, the probability that a household evacuates at time 𝑡, given that it has not evacuated earlier, is:

that vary across 𝑠. These are calibrated using estimates from Gudishala and Wilmot (2012).

and exits from the study area. The joint probability of shelter use and evacuation is:

Although the models in this subsection integrate evacuee compliance behavior into evacuation optimization frameworks, the
following Section 4.2 reviews studies that embed the underlying drivers of compliance directly into the optimization process itself.
4.2. Behavioral driver-based compliance modeling in evacuation optimization
Although most evacuation models represent compliance behavior, particularly by emphasizing the extremes of full compliance and
non-compliance, a subset of studies embeds the underlying factors influencing compliance, such as physical constraints, psychological
states, and structural plan/design features, directly into the optimization framework.
Personal capabilities, particularly mobility limitations and physical vulnerabilities, play a central role in shaping evacuees’ ability
and willingness to comply with evacuation directives. Hsiao et al. (2021), for example, present two optimization models for shelterin-place deployment during dam-break floods, a no-notice scenario where evacuation is restricted to walking. Key decisions include
whether to open a shelter at a given location, which capacity to select (small, medium, or large), and how to assign evacuees, classified
as general public or elderly, to shelters within acceptable walking distances (500m and 300m, respectively). The first model aims
to maximize the total number of evacuees assigned to shelters, where 𝑤𝑘
is the weight assigned to population class 𝑘 to prioritize
vulnerable groups:

extends this by incorporating spatial risk, using 𝑑𝑖

prioritize high-risk areas:

While the first model favors maximizing total coverage with large shelters in dense areas, the second targets high-risk zones
with smaller shelters, enhancing protection for vulnerable groups. By integrating vulnerability weights, spatial risk, and mobility
constraints, this framework supports equitable, accessible evacuation strategies.

present an optimization model for post-disaster evacuation and resettlement, using the 2008 Wenchuan earthquake as context. The
model determines when and where to open shelters, schedule evacuations, assign evacuees, and deploy mental health workers across
disaster sites. At each time 𝑡 and disaster site 𝑠, individuals are classified into three psychological states: susceptible (𝑥

This penalty increases when mental health worker deployment is insufficient relative to evacuee psychological needs. The model
minimizes psychological, deployment, transportation, and shelter costs using a weighted objective. Several concepts regulate psychological dynamics: intervention capacity constraints ensure site-level support limits are not exceeded, while resource constraints
restrict the total number of deployable mental health workers.
To address compliance-influencing factors more holistically, Dulebenets et al. (2020) developed a multi-objective evacuation
planning model that jointly optimizes departure time, route, and shelter assignment to minimize six evacuee burdens: evacuation time
(travel duration), mental demand (cognitive workload), physical demand (discomfort), temporal demand (urgency pressure), effort
(perceived difficulty), and frustration (emotional stress). The model was reformulated into a single-objective function aggregating total
burden, calculated as the number of individuals per vehicle, including carpooling groups, multiplied by the sum of these burdens.
Burdens are quantified using linear regression models based on a driving-simulator study with 115 participants, identifying key
influencing factors: evacuation time is shaped by age, driving frequency, and decision-making speed; mental demand by education,
health, and reaction ability; physical demand by health status, chronic conditions, and vision; temporal demand by time-of-day and
evacuation experience; effort by driving ability and physical condition; and frustration by income, education, and past evacuation
history. Critical constraints ensure that individuals with health or mobility limitations are only assigned to special-needs shelters.
By embedding burden estimation and personal attributes into both objectives and constraints, the model supports human-centered,
behaviorally realistic evacuation planning that enhances compliance and equity.
Beyond personal capabilities and psychological influences, recent studies enhance compliance by embedding structural plan/design features directly into the optimization formulation. Rather than modeling evacuee characteristics, these models provide evacuation plans that are inherently easier to follow, thereby reducing the behavioral gap between planned and realized evacuations.
Even et al. (2015) introduce convergent evacuation plans, in which all assigned routes form a tree converging toward safe zones,
ensuring that evacuees never encounter forks requiring independent route choices. This is achieved by imposing a convergence
constraint that restricts the outdegree of each node to at most one: ∑
𝑒∈𝛿+(𝑖)
𝑥𝑒 ≤ 1 ∀𝑖 ∈ 𝒩 (18)
By eliminating all divergence points from the network, the model removes a documented source of driver hesitation and congestion
during evacuations. Simulations confirm that convergent plans outperform non-convergent alternatives even when hesitation delays
at forks are as small as 0.75 s.
Hafiz Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021) extend this structural approach within a unified zone-based evacuation planning framework, where all evacuees from the same residential zone are assigned a single route. This design is widely adopted by emergency
services to simplify communication and increase compliance. The authors consider three problem-variants: a basic formulation, a
convergent variant imposing the convergence constraint above, and a non-preemptive variant. The non-preemptive formulation is
particularly relevant to compliance, as it ensures that the evacuation of a zone, once initiated, proceeds without interruption at
a constant departure rate, making the schedule easier for authorities to enforce. These findings demonstrate that structural plandesign features, including convergent routing that eliminates driver hesitation at forks and non-preemptive scheduling that simplifies
enforcement, can effectively enhance the realizability of evacuation plans without requiring explicit behavioral modeling of individual evacuees. Building on this framework, Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021) evaluate the same formulations through microscopic
SUMO simulations on a large-scale flood case study in Australia. Their results confirm that plans incorporating convergence and
non-preemption constraints achieve higher fidelity when validated at the microscopic level, despite producing lower theoretical
evacuation rates at the macroscopic level. Extending this perspective to multimodal tsunami evacuation, Bayram et al. (2026) develop a risk-aware mixed-integer second-order cone programming (MISOCP) for Istanbul under full compliance. The model embeds
two structural features aimed at improving plan realizability. First, pedestrian routing is constrained to be convergent, extending the
convergent-tree principle of Even et al. (2015) and Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021) from vehicles to pedestrians. Second, a roadsegment designation mechanism classifies each arc as pedestrian-only, vehicle-only, or mixed-use, eliminating the modal ambiguity
that causes hesitation and unplanned route choices at shared intersections.
Across both subsections, a common methodological characteristic emerges: the optimization models reviewed here incorporate
human behavior through exogenous parameters, fixed compliance assumptions, or externally imposed design constraints, including compliance rates, equilibrium conditions, diversion probabilities, discrete-choice coefficients, mobility limits, psychological cost
terms, and structural plan features. While these approaches offer tractable representations of compliance within mathematical programming frameworks, they do not endogenously couple evacuee decision-making with movement dynamics through interaction
rules or adaptive behavioral mechanisms. This boundary should be recognized when interpreting the scope of optimization-based
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
28

S. Moslemi et al.
Fig. 6. Analytical framework for behaviorally informed evacuation simulation.
contributions to behavioral realism in evacuation planning. The growing integration of simulation techniques, reviewed in the following section, reflects a shift toward capturing more dynamic, emergent, and context-dependent human behaviors, and the combined
use of optimization and simulation frameworks offers promising directions for more realistic and adaptable evacuation models.
5. Simulation-based evacuation models with behavioral representation
Traffic simulation models play a central role in large-scale evacuation planning, enabling systematic analysis of clearance times,
network congestion, and life-safety outcomes under hazardous conditions that cannot be directly observed or experimentally reproduced (Pel et al., 2012). However, the reliability of these analyzes depends on how realistically the underlying models capture evacuee
behavior, understood as a sequence of behavioral responses to authority-issued evacuation decisions, as discussed in Section 3.2. As
demonstrated by large-scale disaster events such as the 2004 Indian Ocean tsunami and Hurricane Katrina, models that fail to account for this behavioral complexity may yield planning recommendations with limited validity and transferability (Mas et al., 2015;
Templeton et al., 2024). To systematically review the literature in this domain, we examine 59 peer-reviewed papers that combine
simulation modeling with explicit behavioral representation in large-scale evacuation contexts. These papers are reviewed through
using the analytical framework shown in Fig. 6 and classified in Table 6.
The framework decomposes the evacuation simulation process into four linked components: inputs and drivers, evacuation decisions, movement and traffic flow, and outputs (Fig. 6). On the input side, the reviewed studies primarily capture two behavior-shaping
factors: risk perception and information, and social influence. These inputs feed into the evacuation decision component, which encompasses the critical decision types discussed in Section 3.2, including protective action, departure time (including pre-evacuation
activities), destination or shelter choice, mode choice, and route choice. The transition from behavioral decision-making to network
loading is mediated by compliance, shown on the link connecting the decision and movement components. Compliance constitutes
the critical interface between behavioral modeling and operational simulation, governing the degree to which prescribed behavior
translates into actual movement demand. The movement and traffic flow component captures how evacuees traverse the system,
whether through vehicular traffic, pedestrian movement, or mixed settings. The outputs component summarizes resulting system
performance across three categories of metrics: traffic efficiency metrics, life-safety outcomes, and behavioral outcomes. Finally, the
network-state feedback arrow identifies studies in which evolving traffic conditions, such as congestion, delay, or route conditions,
update evacuees’ subsequent choices in real-time, closing the loop between behavior and system dynamics.
Based on which framework components each paper engages and how the decision and movement components are coupled, the
59 papers are classified into three structural types. Human-centric (H) models (𝑛 = 9, 15%) engage only the input and decision components without any movement simulation. Integrated one-way (I1) models (𝑛 = 19, 32%) link a behavioral demand model to a
movement simulator unidirectionally: behavioral outputs drive network loading, but prevailing traffic conditions do not feed back
into behavioral decisions during the simulation run. This architecture is operationally tractable and sufficient for evaluating fixed
evacuation plans, but precludes representation of adaptive behavioral responses to observed congestion. Integrated two-way (I2)
models (𝑛 = 31, 53%) implement a network-state feedback loop, allowing dynamic traffic and hazard conditions to update behavioral
decisions in real-time. Key findings from I2 papers confirm that this coupling has material consequences: shelter congestion information redirects destination choice over time, false or corrected information triggers shelter switching mid-evacuation, and congestion
or road damage updates route decisions dynamically (Uenae et al., 2020; Takahashi et al., 2022; Chang et al., 2022, 2024b). However,
the feedback remains partial in most I2 papers, with the majority updating only route choice dynamically while leaving departure
timing and destination selection fixed, which limits the model’s capacity to capture the full co-evolution of behavioral demand and
network supply. The feedback represented in these models operates at the level of aggregate network state, in which congestion, travel
time, road damage, or shelter occupancy inform subsequent decisions. This differs from behavior–motion coupling, in which local
perception, interpersonal interaction, collision avoidance, and speed adaptation jointly determine movement at the individual level.
Within the large-scale evacuation studies reviewed here, no model couples evacuee decision-making with movement dynamics in this
manner, which parallels the boundary identified for optimization models in Section 4.2. The I2 classification should accordingly be
understood as denoting system-level feedback within large-scale network evacuation rather than behavior–motion coupling.
Across all paper types, three methodological categories are used to represent individual evacuation decisions (Table 6). Category 1 (empirical-statistical) generates decisions from models estimated on behavioral survey or revealed-preference data, Category 2
(cognitive-process-based) represents the formation and dynamic updating of decisions through explicit reasoning frameworks, and
Category 3 (rule-assumption-based) approximates decisions through exogenously imposed heuristics. As shown in Fig. 7(a), rulebased methods account for the largest share of implementations (approximately 52%), followed by empirical-statistical approaches
(30%) and cognitive-process-based methods (18%), indicating that the majority of individual decisions in evacuation simulations are

still governed by assumed heuristics rather than behaviorally grounded models. This reliance on rule-based proxies is particularly
problematic in large-scale simulations, where small behavioral inaccuracies at the individual level can compound into large aggregate
errors across networks with hundreds of thousands of agents. However, the dominance of each category varies substantially by decision type. Protective action is the most methodologically balanced decision, with empirical (13), cognitive (12), and rule-based (10)
methods appearing in roughly equal measure. Departure time and route choice are both dominated by rule-based proxies, accounting
for 24 of 38 and 31 of 55 implementations, respectively, which indicates that the decisions most consequential for network loading
are also those least grounded in behavioral evidence. Destination selection similarly relies predominantly on rule-based assignment
(20 of 36), while its empirical share (13) considerably exceeds its cognitive share (three). Mode choice presents a notable gap: while
empirical and rule-based methods are each applied in nine studies, cognitive methods are entirely absent, making it the only decision
type never modeled through a process-based framework across the entire body of reviewed literature. Pre-evacuation activities, covered in only six studies, are primarily addressed through empirical methods (four), with minimal cognitive (one) and rule-based (one)
representation. Non-compliance follows the broader pattern of rule-based dominance (18 of 30), with empirical (6) and cognitive (6)
methods contributing equally but modestly.
The movement simulation component is distributed across three simulation scales, as illustrated in Fig. 7(b). Microscopic simulation models individual entities and their interactions, making it suitable for capturing detailed human behaviors during evacuation
(Dawson et al., 2011). Mesoscopic simulation aggregates individuals with similar behavior to balance realism and computational
efficiency, while still retaining key behavioral patterns without modeling individuals separately (Hoogendoorn and Bovy, 2001).
Macroscopic simulation treats traffic flow at the system level without individual differentiation, offering a more abstract but computationally efficient representation (Hoogendoorn and Bovy, 2001). Among the reviewed papers, microscopic simulation dominates
(𝑛 = 34, including two mixed micro-meso papers). Microscopic resolution, however, spans several distinct modeling paradigms, and
three conceptually different families are represented within this group. The first, traffic microsimulation, resolves individual vehicles
through car-following and lane-changing rules, so that the modeled entity is a vehicle rather than an evacuee. The second, comprising agent-based and GIS-integrated agent-based models, resolves individual evacuees as decision-making agents, although their
movement is typically abstracted to traversal of network links. The third, pedestrian microsimulation, additionally resolves physical
movement dynamics such as local density effects, speed adaptation, and interpersonal spacing. Agent-based formulations account for
the majority of microscopic studies in the reviewed corpus, while pedestrian microsimulation appears in a small number of studies.
Microscopic classification therefore indicates the resolution at which movement is represented, and the distinction among these families identifies the extent to which pedestrian movement behavior is structurally modeled. At this scale, the most frequently applied
methods include agent-based modeling (ABM, 14 papers), traffic microsimulation (TMS, five papers), GIS-integrated ABM (GIS-ABM,
four papers), pedestrian microsimulation (PMS, three papers), and combined pedestrian and flow hybrid frameworks (PMS+FH, two
papers), alongside single-paper applications of MATSim, CFD-ABM, S.ABM, MAS, and ABM+TMS. Mesoscopic models (𝑛 = 7, plus
one meso-macro hybrid) apply methods including the stochastic pedestrian cell transmission model (SPCTM), mesoscopic dynamic
traffic assignment (MESO-DTA), mesoscopic simulation with dynamic traffic assignment (MESO-SIM/DTA), dynamic network loading
and link transmission models (DNL/LTM), queue-based models (QBM), stochastic dynamic traffic assignment (S-DTA), and coupled
agent-based and cell transmission models (ABM+CTM) to large-scale regional vehicular evacuation scenarios where full microscopic
simulation is computationally prohibitive. Macroscopic models (𝑛 = 6) employ dynamic network loading (DNL), mean-field models
(MFM), the four-step traffic model, MATSim, and system-dynamics frameworks, offering analytical tractability suited to studying
policy levers, such as contraflow and departure staging , while nine papers model only the behavioral decision component without
any movement simulation, corresponding to the H-type classification discussed above.
Table 5 summarizes the principal simulation tools available for evacuation modeling, classified by resolution scale; each tool
is cited using its official developer website or technical user manual where available, and for legacy tools or specialized academic
frameworks without active web platforms, the original foundational research articles are provided as primary references.

However, the adoption of these tools across the reviewed studies does not reflect the breadth of available platforms. Custom or
in-house agent-based models are the most frequently adopted overall (15 papers), followed by NetLogo (Wilensky, 1999) (six papers),
AnyLogic (AnyLogic, 2024) (five papers), and MATLAB/Repast (three papers each), while several established tools such as CORSIM,
Paramics, AIMSUN, and PyroSim appear in only one or two studies, indicating substantial room for broader adoption of established
simulation platforms in behavioral evacuation research.
Table 6 classifies the reviewed evacuation simulation studies by author, disaster type, transportation mode, structural type (H, I1,
or I2), influencing factors, simulation scale, simulation method, evacuation decisions modeled, behavioral representation category
(C1, C2, or C3), compliance type, simulation tool, and output. Several descriptive patterns emerge from this classification. In terms of
hazard coverage, hurricane evacuation dominates (𝑛 = 17, 28.81%), followed by general or multi-hazard contexts (𝑛 = 14, 23.73%)
and flood (𝑛 = 9, 15.25%), while rapid-onset hazards such as tsunami, earthquake, and wildfire collectively account for fewer than
15 papers (Fig. 8(a)). This distribution reflects the historical availability of post-event behavioral data in hurricane-prone regions and
implies that behavioral-simulation findings are disproportionately conditioned on slow-onset, vehicle-dominated contexts, limiting
their direct transferability to pedestrian-dominant hazards. Publication volume has grown consistently: 11 papers in 2011–2015,
19 in 2016–2020, and 27 in 2021–2025 (Fig. 8(b)), confirming that behavioral integration in evacuation simulation has become
a mainstream methodological concern over the past decade. In terms of transportation mode, the reviewed studies are distributed
relatively evenly across pedestrian (23.73%), vehicle (22.03%), household (20.34%), mixed-mode (16.95%), and general population
(16.95%) contexts. However, a pronounced asymmetry exists in how each mode is simulated: pedestrian evacuation is modeled almost
exclusively at the microscopic scale (78.57% of pedestrian papers), reflecting the fine-grained spatial and behavioral resolution
required to capture crowd dynamics, exit choice, and density-dependent movement. Vehicular evacuation, by contrast, is more
evenly distributed across microscopic (38.46%), mesoscopic (30.77%), and macroscopic (23.08%) scales, reflecting the wider range
of network sizes and planning horizons addressed in vehicle-based studies.
Compliance behavior is explicitly modeled in 30 of the 59 reviewed papers (51%). Among these, evacuation-order compliance
is the most frequently represented type (19 papers), followed by route compliance (nine papers), while departure-time compliance
and destination compliance each appear in only one paper. This concentration on the binary evacuate-or-stay decision leaves noncompliance with recommended departure schedules, assigned destinations, and designated routes, behaviors widely documented in
post-event surveys, largely unrepresented in current simulation frameworks.
Regarding simulation outputs, traffic efficiency metrics such as clearance time, delay, and network throughput appear in 42
of 59 papers, while behavioral outcomes are reported in 24 and life-safety outcomes in only 10, with 26 papers reporting traffic
metrics exclusively. This imbalance suggests that behavioral representation is primarily used to calibrate demand inputs rather than
to evaluate safety implications. The findings reinforce this concern: evacuation time is highly sensitive to departure timing and
congestion-aware routing (Tamminga et al., 2011; Chang et al., 2024b; Kuligowski et al., 2025), mortality depends critically on
participation rates, milling time, and shelter location (Wang et al., 2016; Mostafizi et al., 2019; Chen et al., 2023; Silva et al., 2025),
and the balance of demand across shelters is strongly affected by information quality and destination assignment rules (Uenae et al.,
2020; Sopha et al., 2021; Takahashi et al., 2022) , yet these safety and behavioral dimensions remain underreported.

Despite the methodological gaps identified throughout this subsection, spanning hazard coverage, decision representation, behavioral grounding, compliance modeling, multimodal interaction, feedback scope, and output emphasis, the reviewed studies collectively
demonstrate that behaviorally informed simulation models capture meaningful interactions between evacuee decisions and system
performance. Yet these studies assess how a given plan performs rather than searching over possible plans to identify which performs
best. Addressing this limitation requires coupling behavioral simulation with optimization frameworks that search over authorityissued decisions while accounting for the adaptive behavioral responses those decisions induce, which we focus in the following
subsection.
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
33

S. Moslemi et al.
Fig. 9. Iterative simulation–optimization methods.
6. Simulation–optimization approaches integrating evacuee behavior considerations
Simulation is not inherently designed to identify optimal decisions in a solution space. Instead, it is primarily used to evaluate
the outcomes of predefined strategies under complex and uncertain conditions. Optimization models, on the other hand, are built
to generate prescriptive solutions. However, their reliance on simplified assumptions often limits their ability to capture system
uncertainty and behavioral complexity. Evacuation behavior clearly illustrates this limitation. Decisions made during emergencies
are influenced by a wide range of psychological, social, experiential, and demographic factors, which are difficult to model using the
simplified abstractions required for tractability in optimization models. While simulation is better suited to reflect these dynamic and
context-dependent aspects of human behavior, it lacks the capacity to prescribe optimal decisions on its own.
To address these limitations, S–O approaches have gained prominence by combining the behavioral realism of simulation with the
prescriptive capabilities of optimization. As shown by Carson and Maria (1997), this integration allows simulation to handle complex
dynamics while optimization guides decision-making.
Literature identifies four coupling frameworks based on the interaction between simulation and optimization: Iterative
Optimization-based Simulation (IOS), Iterative Simulation-based Optimization (ISO), Sequential Simulation-Optimization
(SSO), and Alternating Simulation-Optimization (ASO). These vary by coupling intensity: black-box, grey-box, or integrated. In
ISO, simulation acts as a stochastic black-box evaluator; the optimizer (e.g., GA, PSO) explores the decision space 𝒳 and submits candidate vectors 𝑥 ∈ 𝒳 to receive performance estimates 𝑓(𝑥, 𝜔) without altering the underlying optimization model. Conversely, ASO
creates a bi-directional interaction where simulation outputs dynamically update the optimizer’s constraints or parameters for subsequent iterations, establishing a closed-loop dialogue. IOS employs tight algorithmic coupling with optimization embedded within the
simulation loop at time steps 𝑡, while SSO follows a decoupled pipeline where simulation generates datasets for a separate, subsequent
optimization phase. These distinctions are summarized in Table 7.
A critical distinction in the reviewed literature lies in how human behavior is structurally integrated into these S–O frameworks.
This study distinguishes between parameter-based representations and behavior-oriented modeling. Parameter-based approaches treat
evacuee response as exogenous constants, such as fixed compliance rates or pre-defined reaction times, fed into optimization models.
In contrast, behavior-oriented frameworks employ rule-based or interaction-based mechanisms, where decisions emerge dynamically
from agent interactions during simulation (Nagarajan and Shaw, 2021). While the majority of current S–O studies rely on parameterbased assumptions for computational efficiency, there is an emerging shift toward behavior-oriented coupling in high-fidelity agentbased models. To operationalize these behavioral rules within an S–O framework, the selection of an appropriate simulation fidelity
is paramount.
A comprehensive understanding of S–O requires examining both of its main components: simulation and optimization, each of
which includes multiple methodological variations.
Beyond the traffic-focused tools listed in Section 5, alternative paradigms such as discrete event simulation (DES), system dynamics
(SD), and Monte Carlo simulation (MCS) provide broader perspectives. DES is effective for representing system behavior over time in
movement networks (Na and Banerjee, 2019), while SD supports strategic decision-making in feedback-driven environments (Zhang
et al., 2021). MCS addresses uncertainty through probabilistic sampling, which is critical for the validation and sensitivity analysis
of optimization outputs.
S–O methods are applied iteratively to address complex decision-making, as illustrated in Fig. 9, and Table 7 compares these
frameworks in terms of their functional logic, application contexts, and advantages.
The frameworks summarized in Table 7 offer different trade-offs between computational intensity and model responsiveness. IOS is
particularly suited for real-time operational adjustments where the system must react to emergent behaviors like network congestion.
In contrast, ISO excels in strategic planning where the goal is a robust global solution under fixed stochastic conditions. While SSO
provides a computationally efficient alternative by isolating heavy simulation runs from the optimization—ideal for preliminary
feasibility studies. ASO represents the most flexible integration. Unlike the fixed structures of ISO, ASO’s feedback loop allows the
