From the broader set of studies discussed in Section 2, we focus specifically on those that incorporate S–O approaches. The reviewed
literature is categorized using a comprehensive set of classification criteria, including author names and publication years (Ref.),
simulation-optimization frameworks (Frame.), objective functions (Obj. function), disaster or hazard types (Hazard), solution tools
(Software), and traffic simulator fidelity (Fid.)—microscopic, mesoscopic, or macroscopic. Additional classification criteria include
publishing journals (Journal), human behavior and response during evacuation (Hum. behavior), simulation methods (S.M.)—agentbased, discrete event, or Monte Carlo—and optimization methods (O.M.). Furthermore, the studies are evaluated based on their
integration strategy (Int.), specifically distinguishing between parameter-based (P) and behavior-oriented (B) approaches to reflect
the structural coupling of human dynamics within the S–O framework. Finally, each modeling study is assessed according to the
empirical validation of its behavioral component (E.V.), indicating whether the behavioral parameters were validated against realworld data (Y), partially grounded in empirical evidence (P), or based solely on theoretical assumptions (N). This assessment addresses
the quality of behavioral evidence incorporated in each study, as shown in Table 8.
As shown in Table 8, 74% of the reviewed studies adopt microscopic simulation. Among these, agent-based modeling (ABM) is
the most common technique, applied in 67% of the papers. Mesoscopic and macroscopic simulation each account for only 6% of the
reviewed literature, indicating a strong preference for methods that capture individual-level behaviors.
While the ’H.B.’ (Human Behavior) column identifies the factors considered in the literature, in the vast majority of these cases,
these factors are implemented as exogenous parameters or compliance assumptions rather than dynamic, rule-based decision-motion
coupling mechanisms. Approximately 83% of these reviewed studies utilize parameter-based representations, where behavioral factors
like route choice or compliance are treated as static input variables to maintain computational feasibility. Only 17%, primarily those
utilizing advanced ABS such as Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021) and Cao et al. (2024), transition toward behavior-oriented modeling
by defining explicit interaction rules between individuals and their environment.
Regarding alternative simulation methods, 13% of the reviewed studies employ DES to model human behavior and simulation
optimization. Despite the increase in the total number of papers, the relative share of DES has slightly decreased, as recent studies
such as Islam et al. (2022) and Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021) lean towards custom agent-based frameworks to handle complex
spatial dynamics. None of the reviewed studies use SD for individual behavior modeling. MCS is applied in only 1% of the papers,
primarily for model validation purposes. This shows that researchers mostly prefer methods like ABS and DES that can represent
individual actions and network-level constraints in detail, while SD and MCS are rarely used for modeling human behavior.
Among human behavior factors, route choice remains the most frequently studied, appearing in 58% of the reviewed studies.
This highlights its critical role in mitigating congestion and enhancing overall evacuation efficiency, particularly in agent-based
models where individuals dynamically choose between paths under evolving conditions. Recent studies by Islam et al. (2022) and
Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021) further emphasize how route selection is heavily influenced by real-time congestion and network
delays. Social networks follow at 19%, often captured through herd behavior models or the simulation of group decision-making.
For instance, in the studies by Cheng et al. (2008b) and Bo et al. (2007), social ties are embedded in agent behavior rules, while
Cao et al. (2024) and Romano et al. (2025) have recently demonstrated how peer-to-peer influence significantly alters crowd flow.
Other factors such as panic behavior (10%), transportation systems (12%), evacuation time (6%), and shelter choice (10%) also play
substantial roles. Notably, Chang et al. (2024a) integrated prioritized shelter preferences into the S–O loop, and Hafiz Hasan and
Van Hentenryck (2021) introduced “zonal compliance” as a key behavioral factor in wildfire evacuations. Environmental familiarity
(1%) remains the least explored factor. These findings suggest that while route dynamics and social behavior continue to dominate the

literature, critical dimensions of individual decision-making—such as the psychological impact of panic, environmental familiarity,
and adaptive responses to zonal instructions—require more robust integration into emerging simulation-optimization frameworks.
Approximately 43% of the reviewed studies employ problem-specific heuristics to address complex scenarios, a trend reinforced
by recent models where traditional algorithms struggle with computational scalability (Hasan and Van Hentenryck, 2021; Islam et al.,
2022). Exact methods are used in only 3% of the papers due to their computational intensity. In 26% of the studies, the primary
focus remains on the simulation framework and results rather than on the optimization technique, highlighting a greater emphasis
on application outcomes over methodological advancements.
Regarding optimization techniques, S–O methods incorporate a variety of approaches. Genetic algorithms (GA), used in 7% of the
studies, are favored for their ease of implementation and ability to optimize complex problems (Abdelgawad and Abdulhai, 2010;
Song et al., 2013). Particle swarm optimization (PSO) is applied in 9% of the papers for route optimization (Zong et al., 2014; Swamy
et al., 2017). Tabu search (TS) appears in 3% of the studies. Although simulated annealing (SA) is known for exploring solution
spaces through probabilistic methods, it is not employed in the reviewed evacuation studies due to its limited suitability for dynamic
behavioral models (Amaran et al., 2016). Column generation (CG) is used in 3% of the papers, for evacuation path generation (Cabrera
et al., 2022) and resource allocation (Kim et al., 2017). These findings suggest that problem-specific heuristics and population-based
algorithms are more widely used than exact methods or classical metaheuristics, likely due to their flexibility in adapting to the
complexities of evacuation scenarios.
Among the hybrid methodologies, IOS models, accounting for 19% of the reviewed studies, begin with optimization, followed
by simulation to evaluate the outcomes. These models, typically GIS-based and microscopic, focus on route planning and congestion
management. Cheng et al. (2008b) and Bo et al. (2007) incorporated social network structures to reflect peer influence and group
movement, demonstrating how evacuees tend to follow familiar individuals, thereby affecting traffic dynamics. Wei et al. (2012)
employed a cellular automata-based microscopic model to capture herding behavior and non-compliance, emphasizing the need for
real-time re-optimization as evacuees prioritized peer actions over official instructions. In addition, IOS approaches have also been
applied by (Abdelgawad and Abdulhai, 2010; Zong et al., 2014; Swamy et al., 2017; Alahi Kawsar et al., 2019; Zheng et al., 2021;
Cabrera et al., 2022; Xu et al., 2022a; Zhang et al., 2024a).
ISO stands out as the most widely adopted approach, appearing in 59% of the reviewed studies, primarily because the optimization
process drives the generation of candidate strategies, while simulation acts as a feedback mechanism to evaluate and refine these
strategies. This optimization-driven structure allows ISO to systematically search a wide solution space, incorporate diverse constraints, and adapt decisions based on simulation outputs in large-scale environments. The robustness of this framework is evidenced
by a vast body of literature including Gan et al. (2016), Takabatake et al. (2017), Yuan et al. (2017), Zhang et al. (2009), Afshar and
Haghani (2008), Balakrishna et al. (2008), Chang and Edara (2016, 2018, 2017), Chavhan and Venkataram (2019), Nakanishi et al.
(2020), Wang et al. (2023), Joo et al. (2013), Chen et al. (2006), Cova and Johnson (2002), Lu et al. (2005), Han et al. (2006), Hsu
and Peeta (2013), Chiu et al. (2007), Zou et al. (2005), Sfeir et al. (2018), Li et al. (2022a), Rivers et al. (2014), Song et al. (2013),
Liu et al. (2023), Kimms and Maassen (2011), MA et al. (2013), Chang et al. (2024a), Fu et al. (2014), Huang et al. (2016c), Ma et al.
(2013), Chebika et al. (2025), Yazici and Ozbay (2008), Romano et al. (2025), Nagarajan and Shaw (2021), Takabatake et al. (2026),
Cao et al. (2024), Hafiz Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021), Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021), Islam et al. (2022), Huertas et al.
(2020), Zambrano et al. (2020), Kim et al. (2017). Addressing the scalability limitations of exact mathematical programming, Even
et al. (2015) introduced a two-stage ISO framework that decouples route design from evacuation scheduling. This approach employs a
conflict-aware path-generation heuristic to eliminate delays at intersections, ensuring that optimized plans remain operationally conTransportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
39

S. Moslemi et al.
Fig. 10. Overview of S–O method usage, temporal trends, and disaster types.
vergent and feasible in large-scale urban networks. Representing the urban-scale methodologies, Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021)
demonstrates the pinnacle of ISO application by optimizing zonal evacuation schedules for massive wildfires, where high-fidelity
simulation evaluations are used to ensure the operational feasibility of prescriptive plans in sprawling networks.
SSO models proceed sequentially, first executing simulation to understand system dynamics and then employing optimization.
Lahmar et al. (2006) used CORSIM to adjust routes and control strategies based on congestion. Maassen (2012) combined SUMO
with heuristic optimization to simulate panic-induced disruptions via crowd density thresholds, linking behavioral stress to dynamic
rerouting.
ASO models alternate between simulation and optimization steps, allowing for continuous feedback and adaptive planning. Liu
et al. (2016) developed an agent-based model in NetLogo to capture decentralized decisions and spontaneous rerouting during nonotice evacuations.
Regarding S–O method types, Table 8 and Fig. 10(a) show that 13 studies used IOS, 41 applied ISO, two employed SSO, and one
used ASO. The remaining 12 studies are methodological reviews that do not implement a specific simulation-optimization coupling
framework.
The dominance of the ISO framework, accounting for 59% of the reviewed studies, stems from its superior ability to bridge
the gap between prescriptive optimization and stochastic simulation. Unlike sequential models, ISO’s iterative feedback loop allows
for a systematic search of wide solution spaces while simultaneously incorporating granular human behaviors. This scalability and
flexibility make it the preferred choice for recent large-scale applications, where simulation-based performance metrics are used to
continuously refine optimization parameters (Hasan and Van Hentenryck, 2021; Islam et al., 2022). The robustness of this approach
is further demonstrated in high-demand scenarios involving up to 0.1 million agents, proving its unique suitability for modeling
complex network constraints and real-time behavioral adaptations in large-scale emergency contexts (Even et al., 2015; Kim et al.,
2017; Huertas et al., 2020).
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
40

S. Moslemi et al.
As shown in Fig. 10(b), interest in S–O approaches has grown significantly, with 59% of the 69 reviewed studies published between
2016 and 2025. This upward trend reflects a growing emphasis on urban-scale, data-driven, computational methods that combine
optimization and simulation to improve network-level evacuation planning and management and disaster response. The recent surge
in publications totaling 41 studies in the final period shows the need for interdisciplinary collaboration across engineering, behavioral
sciences, and emergency evacuation planning and management to develop more adaptive and effective large-scale evacuation strategies. As illustrated in Fig. 10(c), hurricane scenarios dominate the reviewed literature, accounting for 47% of all studies, followed by
general or multi-hazard contexts at 16% and wildfire/fire at 12%. This distribution reflects the historically high research attention toward large-scale, notice-based evacuations, while flood, tsunami, and earthquake scenarios remain comparatively underrepresented,
highlighting clear opportunities for future simulation-optimization research in these hazard domains.
7. Empirical validation of behavioral assumptions
Optimization-Based Evacuation Models. The empirical grounding of behavioral components in the reviewed optimization models
varies considerably. Most SO and UE-based formulations rely on established traffic assignment theory without validating compliance
assumptions against observed evacuation data. Among partial-compliance models, Yi et al. (2017) calibrate behavioral parameters using empirically estimated discrete choice models, and Sun et al. (2017) derive response rates from behavioral research and
expert-input, while others rely on randomly generated deviations (e.g., Lessan and Kim, 2022), theoretically-assumed fairness thresholds (e.g., Bayram et al., 2015; Bayram and Yaman, 2018a,b), endogenously computed but uncalibrated compliance rates (Zeng
et al., 2016), or assumption-based attractiveness parameters (Wang and Wallace, 2022; Xu et al., 2025). Hsu and Peeta (2014)
estimate their route choice model from synthetic behavioral data rather than real-world observations. Among models embedding
compliance-enhancing factors, Dulebenets et al. (2020) calibrate their parameters through a driving-simulator experiment with 115
participants, whereas Hu et al. (2014) employ theoretically motivated but uncalibrated panic dynamics. Even et al. (2015) validate
plan performance through mesoscopic simulations, while Hasan and Van Hentenryck (2021) provide microscopic validation using the
SUMO traffic simulator, both offering indirect empirical support for structural plan-design features. While a few optimization studies
demonstrate meaningful empirical grounding through calibrated choice models, simulator experiments, or microscopic validation,
the majority of behavioral components remain theoretically assumed.
Simulation-Based Evacuation Models. A similar pattern emerges in the simulation literature, where the empirical grounding of
behavioral components follows a clear hierarchy tied to the three methodological categories. Empirical-statistical models (Category
1) provide the strongest foundation, deriving parameters from post-event surveys or revealed-preference data. Cognitive-processbased models (Category 2) are theoretically grounded but typically calibrated against limited or context-specific evidence. Ruleassumption-based models (Category 3), which constitute the largest share, impose exogenous heuristics without calibration against
observed behavioral data. This imbalance is most pronounced for the decisions with the greatest operational consequences: departure
time and route choice are predominantly governed by rule-based proxies, while protective action is the only decision type with
roughly balanced representation across all three categories. Mode choice is never modeled through a cognitive framework, and
non-compliance is dominated by rule-based assumptions. While meaningful empirical progress has been made for protective action
decisions, departure time and route choice, which are the most consequential decisions for network-level performance, remain largely
governed by assumed heuristics.
Simulation-Optimization Based Evacuation Models. These frameworks inherit behavioral assumptions from both their simulation and optimization components, and as the preceding discussion indicates, neither component is typically well-calibrated. Since
simulation-optimization models do not generally introduce additional empirical validation of these inherited assumptions, they face
a compound validity challenge. Across the reviewed studies, these findings converge into a consistent picture. Only a very small proportion (3%) provides strong empirical validation by calibrating simulation kernels with real-world disaster data, such as observed
traffic counts from actual hurricane events (Chang and Edara, 2017, 2018). A further 18% offers partial validation by grounding
selected behavioral parameters in empirical evidence. For example, departure time distributions derived from post-event surveys
(Takabatake et al., 2017; Nakanishi et al., 2020), demand curves calibrated against historical traffic observations (Yazici and Ozbay,
2008; Cova and Johnson, 2002), or compliance rates informed by prior field studies (Hafiz Hasan and Van Hentenryck, 2021). However, the large majority (79%) rely entirely on synthetic behavioral proxies, such as idealized full compliance or fixed herd-following
rules, without any empirical calibration (Zhang et al., 2009; Liu et al., 2023; Nagarajan and Shaw, 2021). This persistent validity gap
across optimization, simulation, and simulation-optimization frameworks suggests that while these studies contribute significantly
to methodological advancement, their operational reliability in real-world large-scale evacuations remains fundamentally limited by
the lack of behavioral validation. Closing this gap will require systematic integration of empirically calibrated behavioral models into
evacuation planning frameworks.
8. Conclusion and future research directions
Effective evacuation is vital for reducing disaster risk, as climate change, rapid urbanization, and global digital interconnectivity
increase the frequency and complexity of emergencies. However, evacuation strategies are often designed under the assumption of
full public compliance, despite the frequent occurrence of non-compliance, creating significant challenges for planners and authorities. To address this reality, this study comprehensively reviews research on integrating human behavior into large-scale evacuation
modeling. Understanding how individuals respond to key decisions made by authorities, and the factors influencing those responses,
provides the foundation for realistic and actionable planning. Building on this, the study examines two complementary dimensions:
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
41

S. Moslemi et al.
the behavioral perspective, which identifies drivers of evacuee compliance, and the modeling perspective, which embeds these insights into evacuation optimization, simulation, and simulation–optimization frameworks. Beyond synthesizing prior work, the study
evaluates its progress and limitations in capturing real-world complexities, including uncertainty, equity, real-time data integration,
and the protection of vulnerable populations. Finally, it highlights opportunities to integrate advanced methodologies, such as digital technologies, artificial intelligence, and other emerging tools, and to translate academic research into operational practice for
data-driven and actionable evacuation planning.
8.1. Key insights from the literature
Research on influencing factors in evacuation behavior is extensive and highlights key insights. Factors such as demographic
characteristics and family context are the most frequently studied and shape evacuation decisions. Combined with decision-related
studies, these investigations demonstrate progress in understanding compliance behavior across critical decisions points. Most research focuses on route choice, departure timing, destination selection, and protective actions, particularly evacuation behavior.
Identifying the factors guiding choices is essential for developing strategies that enhance adherence to instructions and translate
behavioral insights into actionable evacuation plans.
Most optimization models integrating compliance behavior focus on routing and adopt idealized, binary assumptions: SO for full
compliance, UE for non-compliance, and CSO models that add behavioral realism through fairness constraints, assuming evacuees
comply fully once these are met. CSO models represent the first progress toward behaviorally aware optimization. More recent studies
explicitly address partial compliance, often for specific disasters and incorporating uncertainty, revealing the operational impacts of
varying compliance rates and producing more realistic and equitable solutions. A smaller group of models embeds determinants of
compliance, such as physical or psychological considerations, directly into optimization frameworks, offering solutions that are both
realistic and equitable.
Simulation-based studies demonstrate that behaviorally informed models capture meaningful interactions between evacuee decisions and system performance, and the growing volume of research in this area confirms that behavioral integration has become
a mainstream methodological concern in evacuation simulation. Two-way integrated models (network-state feedback) which allow
evolving network and hazard conditions, such as congestion or blockages, to dynamically update individual choices, produce substantively different outcomes compared to static or one-way architectures, with direct implications for shelter management, route
guidance, and real-time planning. Across all three structural categories discussed in Section 5, rule-based methods dominate how individual decisions are represented, and this reliance is most pronounced for departure timing and route choice. This creates a notable
tension: the decisions with the greatest influence on network loading are precisely those least grounded in behavioral evidence.
Simulation–optimization approaches build on these developments by combining the prescriptive strength of optimization with
the ability of simulation to capture complex, dynamic processes. ISO methods are especially effective, systematically exploring wide
solution spaces and adapting decisions based on simulation outputs. Similar to influencing-factor studies and optimization models,
S–O approaches often prioritize route choice as the key decision variable, emphasizing its critical role in mitigating congestion and
improving evacuation efficiency.
8.2. Gaps and opportunities for future research
Although recent advances highlight promising progress in integrating behavioral insights into both optimization and S–O frameworks, our synthesis also reveals several critical gaps. Addressing them is essential to move beyond models toward behaviorally
realistic, adaptive, and actionable approaches for real-world crises.
• Behavioral Coverage Across Decisions: Existing behavioral studies and modeling frameworks focus predominantly on route
choice, while transportation mode is rarely addressed and road-segment designation is almost absent. Most of the routing studies
prioritize vehicle-based evacuation and overlook multimodal strategies, despite the operational challenges of mixed traffic. This
lack of road-segment assignment for managing mixed flows also leaves non-compliance behavior underexplored. In the simulation literature, this imbalance is equally pronounced: mode choice has never been modeled through a cognitive or process-based
framework, and road-segment designation is entirely absent from simulation-based studies. Furthermore, protective actions such
as shelter-in-place and shadow evacuation receive minimal attention. Future research should therefore adopt an integrated framework that captures a broader range of decisions such as transportation mode selection, road-segment designation, and diverse
protective responses to better reflect real-world evacuation behavior.
• Modeling Compliance and Its Drivers: Partial compliance appears in only a few optimization studies, typically for a single
decision type, and these often rely on simplified or randomly generated behavioral inputs. Moreover, only a limited number of
models embed underlying drivers of compliance, such as physical factors like mobility constraints for vulnerable populations
or psychological considerations. In the simulation literature, compliance modeling is similarly limited, remaining concentrated
on the binary evacuate-or-stay decision while non-compliance with recommended departure schedules, assigned destinations,
and designated routes, behaviors widely documented in post-event surveys, is largely unrepresented. More broadly, rule-based
methods dominate how individual decisions are represented across all simulation categories, particularly for departure timing
and route choice, which remain the least grounded in behavioral evidence. Our quality-of-evidence assessment further reveals a
critical validity gap” in the literature, where approximately 59% of the reviewed S–O frameworks rely on synthetic or unvalidated
behavioral proxies. This heavy reliance on theoretical assumptions, rather than empirically calibrated kernels, limits the operational reliability of prescriptive evacuation strategies. To advance this area, future research should integrate influencing factors
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
42

S. Moslemi et al.
with partial compliance into unified frameworks, prioritizing the transition from “synthetic behavior” to “calibrated behavior”
by utilizing real-world datasets such as GPS trajectories, video analytics, and post-disaster surveys to ensure that optimization
outputs are grounded in observed human reality.
• Hazard Types and Geographic Contexts: Most of the reviewed studies focus on hurricanes in the United States, leaving suddenonset hazards, such as tsunamis, earthquakes, and wildfires, as well as compound disasters, underexplored. This U.S.-centric focus
also means that regional differences in infrastructure, governance, and culture remain largely unaddressed. Expanding research
to cover a wider range of hazards and geographic contexts is therefore essential for developing globally relevant and life-saving
evacuation strategies.
• Equity and Vulnerability Considerations: Vulnerable groups, including the elderly, disabled, low-income populations, and
those without access to private vehicles, are rarely incorporated into optimization or simulation–optimization models. While a
few studies explore their destination and mode choices or prioritize them through mobility constraints, most models focus on
average population behavior, leaving the specific challenges of these groups underrepresented. This limitation is compounded by
the predominant focus on traffic efficiency metrics in simulation outputs, while life-safety and compliance-driven consequences
remain underreported, leaving the field without adequate tools to assess whether evacuation strategies achieve their primary goal
of saving lives and serving all population groups equitably. Future research should explicitly integrate vulnerable populations into
evacuation modeling and planning to ensure strategies that are both equitable and effective.
• Real-Time Data Integration and Human-in-the-Loop Approaches: Most existing models are static and cannot ingest live data
or adapt strategies as a disaster unfolds, despite the highly dynamic nature of emergencies. This limitation is evident in the
simulation literature, where most two-way integrated models dynamically update only route choice, leaving departure timing,
destination selection, and mode choice predetermined, which limits representation of how evacuees adapt under evolving conditions. Extending dynamic feedback mechanisms to jointly update multiple decision dimensions is a critical step toward more
adaptive simulation architectures. Future models should incorporate real-time data streams, including ITS, IoT sensors, satellite
imagery, mobile devices, and social media, and support human-in-the-loop decision-making so that strategies can be adjusted
interactively, enabling adaptive and resilient evacuation planning. More fundamentally, feedback in existing models operates at
the level of network state rather than through behavior–motion coupling. Representing local perception, interpersonal interaction,
and speed adaptation within large-scale evacuation models remains an open methodological challenge.
• Uncertainty in Evacuation Planning: Evacuation planning is characterized by deep uncertainty arising from complex human
behavior, rapidly changing environmental conditions, and incomplete or imperfect information. While some optimization models
that include compliance consider uncertainty, mostly using stochastic approaches, most of simulation–optimization models remain
deterministic, relying on simplifying assumptions misaligned with real-world conditions. Robust-optimization approaches can be
effectively used to address this critical issue, which is mostly overlooked in the existing literature. Future research towards this
direction can have a very significant practical impact to offer stronger performance in ensuring reliable evacuation planning.
• Integration of Emerging Technologies and Advanced Methods: Digital technologies, including AI, machine learning, ITS,
digital twins, and satellite imagery, are still underutilized in evacuation modeling (Bayram, 2024) .
A Digital Twin is defined as a dynamic virtual replica of a physical system, such as an urban road network, synchronized
through real-time data exchange. Rather than serving merely as a 3D visualization tool, a DT can function as a bi-directional synchronization platform that continuously integrates live data streams from ITS, mobile devices, IoT sensors, drones, and satellites.
Evacuation planning can embrace this technology by shifting from static models to live-responsive systems where a DT acts as a
high-fidelity simulation engine, enabling planners to test and deploy optimization strategies in a virtual environment that mirrors
the real-time state of the physical city, while providing real-time situational awareness and dynamic routing recommendations as
events evolve. ITS and IoT sensor networks can enable real-time traffic and hazard data collection during evacuations, which, combined with machine learning and data analytics, can be transformed into dynamic route guidance and congestion management
decisions (Bo et al., 2007). Autonomous- and connected-vehicle technologies can further support coordinated, system-optimal
routing by centralized traffic management authorities (Fagnant and Kockelman, 2015; Mahmassani, 2016). Satellite imagery and
remote sensing can complement these tools by enabling rapid post-disaster damage assessment and real-time updates to evacuation network models (Joyce et al., 2009). These tools can enrich behavioral prediction, enable real-time traffic assignment, detect
blocked roads or hazards, and support congestion management. Methodologically, hybrid models that combine agent-based simulation with robust-optimization, stochastic programming, or reinforcement learning offer substantial potential but are rarely
applied.
• Strengthening Academic–Practitioner Collaboration: Progress is limited by a persistent gap between academic research and
operational practice. In the simulation domain, this gap is further reflected in the heavy reliance on custom or in-house agentbased models, with many established simulation platforms rarely adopted, pointing to a need for greater standardization and
reproducibility. Collaborative, co-designed modeling efforts involving local emergency managers, transportation agencies, NGOs,
community organizations, and international bodies (e.g., UNDRR, IFRC) are essential. In addition to high-level agencies like
FEMA or FHWA, partnerships with local authorities and grassroots organizations will enable models that reflect diverse realities.
Standardized datasets, transparent modeling protocols, and interoperable simulation platforms can help bridge this divide and
ensure that models are directly usable in practice.
• Reframing Evacuation as a Socio-Technical Challenge: Evacuation is not solely a logistics problem but a complex sociotechnical system shaped by human factors and infrastructure. Future research should integrate behavioral science, communication,
sociology, and ethics into the design of algorithms and models. Such multidisciplinary approaches will produce tools that are not
only accurate but also fair, trusted, and implementable in real-world decision environments.
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
43

S. Moslemi et al.
Together, these gaps highlight a research agenda where computational rigor must be matched with behavioral realism, inclusivity, and practical insights. Advancing in these directions will require a new generation of adaptive, data-rich, and human-centered
evacuation models that are designed hand-in-hand with practitioners, rather than in isolation from them.
8.3. Bridging the academic–practitioner divide
Evacuation planning and management cannot remain a purely academic exercise. The complex realities of large-scale emergencies
demand stronger, continuous collaboration between researchers, operational agencies, local authorities, international organizations,
NGOs, and community stakeholders. While academic research has generated increasingly sophisticated models, these tools will only
achieve real impact if they are co-designed, validated, and adapted with those responsible for decision-making in the field.
The framework proposed by Lindell et al. (2018) shows this principle: effective evacuation planning integrates engineering, social
science, emergency management, and policy insights. Building on this perspective, our review highlights several priorities for closing
the gap between research and practice:
Practitioners need:
• Practical tools grounded in validated models and informed by reliable, real-world data.
• Strategies tailored to context, recognizing that tsunami, earthquake, wildfire, hurricane, or compound hazards require distinct
approaches.
• Guidance on public communication, coordination of multimodal transportation, and adaptive shelter allocation under deep uncertainty.
Academics must:
• Ground their models in empirical-behavioral data and stakeholder feedback rather than relying on simplified assumptions.
• Design for usability, transparency, and operational constraints to ensure models can be applied in the field.
• Extend collaboration beyond U.S.-based agencies (e.g., FEMA, FHWA) to include diverse organizations such as Turkey’s Disaster
and Emergency Management Presidency (AFAD), Japan’s Cabinet Office, European Civil Protection, local municipalities, and
community groups.
Academic–practitioner partnerships need to evolve from one-off consultations into long-term, collaborations. Early and sustained
involvement of practitioners in model design, scenario development, data collection, and validation is essential. Approaches such
as co-designed simulations, stakeholder workshops, and post-event reviews can ensure that models reflect operational realities and
build trust for real-world adoption.
The rapid expansion of digital technologies, including ITS, mobile data, social media, IoT sensors, and satellite imagery, creates unprecedented opportunities for real-time coordination between modelers and field-level responders. Decision-support systems should
move beyond prescribing shortest paths toward recommending strategies that balance efficiency, safety, equity, and feasibility given
current behavioral and infrastructure conditions. Advanced approaches such as reinforcement learning, and dynamic traffic assignment under partial compliance will only reach their potential when combined with the expertise of practitioners who understand
local-level constraints and priorities.
Bridging this divide requires institutional mechanisms for sustained dialogue, shared data platforms, joint funding initiatives, and
multidisciplinary research programs. By fostering these collaborations, models can evolve from academic prototypes into trusted,
actionable systems that help protect lives during crises.
8.4. Toward a multidisciplinary, inclusive research agenda
The next generation of evacuation modeling will depend on interdisciplinary collaboration, bringing together operations research,
transportation engineering, behavioral science, data analytics, artificial intelligence, and emergency management practice. The goal
is to build models that are scientifically rigorous, behaviorally authentic, technologically advanced, and directly usable in decisionmaking environments.
Key research priorities include:
• AI-enabled adaptive decision support: Leveraging machine learning and reinforcement learning for adaptive routing and shelter
allocation that responds to uncertainty, partial compliance, and changing conditions on the ground. These methods can also learn
from past evacuation data to anticipate likely human responses.
• Inclusive and climate-resilient planning: Placing equity and inclusivity at the core of model design to ensure that strategies
account for vulnerable populations, multimodal mobility, and the disproportionate risks posed by climate-related and compound
disasters in different regions of the world.
• Open data and benchmarking: Promoting standardized, openly available datasets, common benchmarks, and replicable scenarios to make models comparable across hazards and regions, accelerating innovation and enabling independent validation.
• Institutionalized co-design: Establishing long-term, multidisciplinary research programs where academics, practitioners, and
policymakers jointly design, test, and refine evacuation models, ensuring that methods remain relevant and trusted in real-world
settings.
Transportation Research Part B 215 (2027) 103611
44

S. Moslemi et al.
8.5. Final remarks
This review calls for a fundamental rethinking of how evacuations are modeled, simulated, and managed. They must no longer
be treated solely as technical optimization problems, but as human-centered, socio-technical challenges shaped by the interplay of
human behavior, institutional constraints, and rapidly advancing digital technologies. The evidence synthesized here highlights both
how far the field has come and how much more remains to be done to align models with the realities of complex, fast-evolving crises.
Ultimately, embedding human behavior into evacuation modeling is not only a technical challenge but also a societal responsibility. Building the next generation of evacuation systems will require sustained collaboration between researchers, local authorities,
emergency managers, and communities across diverse hazard types and geographic contexts. Only by combining rigorous methods
with inclusive, real-time, and practitioner-informed approaches can evacuation models evolve into trusted decision-support systems
that protect those most at risk and function effectively under the pressures of real disasters.

Appendix A. Classification of factors affecting evacuee behavior
Based on the classification by Lindell et al. (2018) and Ibrahim et al. (2016), evacuation behavior is shaped by three main domains: environmental, social, and personal factors. Environmental factors include disaster severity, time of occurrence, weather,
infrastructure, proximity, and accessibility—conditions that affect perceived risk and the logistical feasibility of evacuation. Social
factors relate to the influence of social networks, cultural norms, language barriers, and the communication of warnings through
trusted sources and clear channels, that guide information processing and compliance. Personal characteristics encompass health status, psychological responses, economic resources, family structure, such as the presence of children or pets, prior disaster experience,
and demographics such as age, gender, education, and length of residence—factors that influence both capacity and motivation to
evacuate.
